Health & disease Diseases Avian influenza Iowa State University's H5N1 study highlights need for broader livestock surveillance Researchers say more monitoring is needed after finding mammary glands of several livestock species could harbor avian influenza 12 August 2026 2 minute read By: Iowa State University North America An ongoing outbreak of highly pathogenic avian influenza has affected more than 184 million domestic poultry since 2022 and, since making the leap to dairy cattle in spring 2024, more than 1,000 milking cow herds. A new study led by Iowa State University researchers shows that the mammary glands of several other production animals – including pigs, sheep, goats, beef cattle and alpacas – are biologically suitable to harbor avian influenza, due to high levels of sialic acids. “The main thing we wanted to understand in this study is whether there is potential for transmission among these other domestic mammals and humans, and it looks like there is,” said Rahul Nelli, the study’s lead author and a research assistant professor of veterinary diagnostic and production animal medicine. Sialic acid, a sugar molecule found on the surface of many types of animal cells, provides an influenza virus the microscopic docking station it needs to infect a host cell, an entry point for attaching and invading. A study by many of the same researchers last year found that dairy cattle udders have high levels of sialic acid, which helped explain why the H5N1 avian influenza outbreak was able to spread rapidly among dairy herds. In the study published Nov. 27 in the Journal of Dairy Science, a research team that includes scientists from the ISU College of Veterinary Medicine and the U.S. Department of Agriculture’s National Animal Disease Center in Ames also found the same receptors in the mammary glands of the humans. Only a few sporadic cases of H5N1 infection have been reported in the animals examined in the new study, but those species aren’t being tested on a widespread basis, said Dr. Todd Bell, professor of veterinary pathology and a study co-author. “If we don’t look, we don’t know,” Bell said. In dairy herds, H5N1 infections are causing sick cows to produce milk contaminated with the virus, prompting nationwide surveillance testing of raw cow milk samples by the USDA. Pasteurization kills influenza viruses, so store-bought milk is safe. But concerns about raw milk should extend to other mammalian livestock, Nelli said. “Some people do consume the raw milk of these other animals,” he said. The presence of the virus in milk from infected cows has likely played a role in the H5N1 spreading and makes transmission to humans a bigger risk, Nelli said. “If a virus in livestock is being spread by respiratory infections, few humans will be in close enough contact to catch it. But milk is an entirely different situation because it’s transported into communities,” he said. All of the mammary gland tissues examined in the new study had sialic acid receptors preferred by both avian influenza and the seasonal influenza that circulates more readily among humans. The possibility of both types of viruses comingling and transmitting between different species heightens concerns about more dangerous adaptations emerging, Bell said. H5N1 has in the past had a fatality rate in humans of around 50%, though the 71 confirmed human infections during the current outbreak have led to just two deaths. “We need to try to stay ahead of this so it doesn’t have a chance to continue to replicate and potentially evolve into something even more troublesome,” he said. Event
A peer-reviewed study led by Iowa State University researchers found that mammary glands of multiple livestock species possess sialic acid receptors that make them biologically suitable hosts for avian influenza, including H5N1. The study highlights potential transmission risks to humans via raw milk and calls for broader livestock surveillance.
Scale, impact and risk

Only sporadic H5N1 cases reported in examined animals; human infections during current outbreak: 71 confirmed with 2 deaths. Pasteurized milk is safe; raw milk from infected cows may be contaminated. Risk of viral reassortment between avian and seasonal influenza in mammals heightens concerns about dangerous adaptations.

Unresolved: extent of H5N1 presence in non-dairy livestock and human transmission risk via raw milk from other species.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

澳大利亚将为脆弱的本地鸟类接种 H5N1 禽流感疫苗 - Anadolu Ajansı

Australia to vaccinate vulnerable native birds against H5N1 bird flu - Anadolu Ajansı
新闻简报Australia has announced plans to vaccinate vulnerable native bird species in captivity against H5N1 bird flu, as the virus continues to spread across the country. More than 230 cases have been confirmed nationwide, marking the first mass wildlife mortality event linked to the virus. The government has secured an initial 2 million Australian dollars ($1.3 million) worth of vaccines, with the Agriculture Minister stating that vaccination is part of the response to protect species most at risk. However, Chief Veterinary Officer Beth Cookson cautioned that vaccination is not a standalone solution and emphasized the importance of biosecurity measures. Widespread vaccination of wild birds is considered impractical due to the vaccine requiring two doses, and only jurisdictions like Tasmania, the Northern Territory, and the Australian Capital Territory remain without confirmed cases. The initiative aims to mitigate the impact of H5N1 on native bird populations while addressing the broader spread of the virus.
查看新闻五要素
时间
当局将根据哪些物种对病毒的脆弱性以及对保护的潜在影响来确定哪些物种需要接种疫苗。
地点与对象
澳大利亚各州和地区都有脆弱的本土鸟类,塔斯马尼亚州、北领地和澳大利亚首都地区仍没有确诊病例。
事件
据当地媒体报道,随着病毒继续在全国范围内传播,澳大利亚将开始为圈养的脆弱本地鸟类接种 H5N1 禽流感疫苗。
规模、影响与风险
全国确诊病例超过 230 例,这是澳大利亚首例与该病毒有关的大规模野生动物 死亡率 事件。尚未报告在家禽或更广泛的农业中检测到 H5N1。
应对、状态与不确定性
疫苗接种 不是一个独立的解决方案,生物安全措施仍然至关重要。广泛使用 野生鸟类 的 疫苗接种 是不切实际的,因为 疫苗 需要两剂。

Australia to vaccinate vulnerable native birds against H5N1 bird flu - Anadolu Ajansı

News Brief
Australia has announced plans to vaccinate vulnerable native bird species in captivity against H5N1 bird flu, as the virus continues to spread across the country. More than 230 cases have been confirmed nationwide, marking the first mass wildlife mortality event linked to the virus. The government has secured an initial 2 million Australian dollars ($1.3 million) worth of vaccines, with the Agriculture Minister stating that vaccination is part of the response to protect species most at risk. However, Chief Veterinary Officer Beth Cookson cautioned that vaccination is not a standalone solution and emphasized the importance of biosecurity measures. Widespread vaccination of wild birds is considered impractical due to the vaccine requiring two doses, and only jurisdictions like Tasmania, the Northern Territory, and the Australian Capital Territory remain without confirmed cases. The initiative aims to mitigate the impact of H5N1 on native bird populations while addressing the broader spread of the virus.
Original source text (English)
Asia - Pacific Australia to vaccinate vulnerable native birds against H5N1 bird flu More than 230 cases confirmed nationwide as country records first mass wildlife mortality event Faisal Mahmud 11 August 2026 • Update : 11 August 2026 ISLAMABAD Australia will begin vaccinating vulnerable native bird species in captivity against the deadly H5N1 bird flu as the virus continues to spread across the country, local media reported Tuesday. More than 230 cases have been confirmed nationwide, while Australia recorded its first mass wildlife mortality event linked to the virus last week, according to ABC News. Agriculture Minister Julie Collins said the vaccination campaign would begin across states and territories in the coming days. “We cannot stop the H5 bird flu spreading amongst wild birds, but we can take action to protect the species most at risk and vaccination is now part of that response,” Collins said. Authorities will determine which species are vaccinated based on their vulnerability to the virus and the potential impact on conservation. Environment Minister Murray Watt said widespread vaccination of wild birds was impractical because the vaccine requires two doses, although authorities could vaccinate wild birds where feasible. The government has secured an initial 2 million Australian dollars ($1.3 million) worth of vaccines. Chief Veterinary Officer Beth Cookson cautioned that vaccination was “not a standalone solution,” stressing that biosecurity measures remained crucial. Tasmania, the Northern Territory and the Australian Capital Territory remain the only jurisdictions without confirmed cases. Authorities have reported no H5N1 detections in poultry or the broader agricultural industry. Time
Authorities will determine which species are vaccinated based on their vulnerability to the virus and the potential impact on conservation.
Location and population
Australia, with vulnerable native bird species across states and territories, and Tasmania, the Northern Territory, and the Australian Capital Territory remaining without confirmed cases.
Event
Australia will begin vaccinating vulnerable native bird species in captivity against H5N1 bird flu, as the virus continues to spread across the country, reported by local media.
Scale, impact and risk
More than 230 cases confirmed nationwide, marking Australia's first mass wildlife mortality event linked to the virus. No H5N1 detections in poultry or the broader agricultural industry have been reported.
Response, status and uncertainty
Vaccination is not a standalone solution, and biosecurity measures remain crucial. Widespread vaccination of wild birds is impractical due to the vaccine requiring two doses.

newsGP - 禽流感对人类的威胁“非常低”:CSIRO - 澳大利亚皇家全科医生学院 (RACGP)

newsGP - Bird flu threat ‘very low’ for humans: CSIRO - Royal Australian College of General Practitioners (RACGP)
新闻简报自 2026 年 8 月该国首次记录到大规模鸟类 死亡率 事件后,澳大利亚联邦科学与工业研究组织 (CSIRO) 澳大利亚疾病预防中心 (ACDP) 的专家评估了 H5N1 禽流感 传播 从鸟类传播到人类的风险“非常低”。尚未发现与 暴发 有关的人类病例,大多数国际人类 H5N1 病例均为轻度病例,严重病例仅限于老年人或易感人群。当局正在维多利亚州为圈养的本土鸟类和大约 5,000 只小企鹅接种疫苗,以降低预计的死亡率。全 基因组 测序正在进行中,以追踪病毒变化和局部 传播,同时建议全科医生监测有流感样症状和鸟类接触的患者。季节性流感 疫苗接种 建议高危职业群体使用,以降低混合感染风险,但它不能提供针对人畜共患菌株的保护。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 8 月 12 日发布;活动日期为 2026 年 8 月。
地点与对象
澳大利亚(西澳大利亚州、维多利亚州);受影响的种群包括圈养的本地鸟类、菲利普岛和圣基尔达(维多利亚州)的小企鹅以及职业暴露的人类。
事件
在澳大利亚首次记录大规模鸟类 死亡率 事件后,澳大利亚卫生当局和 CSIRO 专家评估 H5N1 禽流感 死亡率 从鸟类传播到人类的风险“非常低”。据报道,该事件正在调查中并持续监测。
规模、影响与风险
尚未记录到与当前澳大利亚 暴发 相关的 H5N1 人类病例。国际上,大多数人类H5N1病例都是轻度呼吸道感染或结膜炎;严重病例与老年人或患有易感疾病的人有关。英国对 428 名职业接触者进行的一项研究发现,只有不到 1% 的人符合感染标准,其中有 2 例确诊为轻症病例,没有重症病例。联邦政府计划 疫苗接种 圈养本土鸟类;维多利亚计划为大约 5000 只小企鹅接种疫苗。
应对、状态与不确定性
CSIRO 的澳大利亚疾病预防中心 (ACDP) 正在进行全 基因组 测序,以追踪病毒起源、检测变化、表征当地 传播 并监测与流行的澳大利亚 禽流感 病毒的重配。尽管 传播 风险较低,但当局仍强调保持警惕和高度戒备状态。建议全科医生依靠流行病学信息作为指导,特别是对于有流感样症状和鸟类接触史的患者。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

newsGP - Bird flu threat ‘very low’ for humans: CSIRO - Royal Australian College of General Practitioners (RACGP)

News Brief
Australian experts from the CSIRO’s Australian Centre for Disease Preparedness (ACDP) have assessed the risk of H5N1 avian influenza transmission from birds to humans as 'very low' following the country’s first recorded mass bird mortality event in August 2026. No human cases linked to the outbreak have been detected, and most international human H5N1 cases have been mild, with severe cases confined to elderly or predisposed individuals. Authorities are vaccinating captive native birds and approximately 5,000 little penguins in Victoria to mitigate predicted mortalities. Whole-genome sequencing is underway to track viral changes and local transmission, while GPs are advised to monitor patients with flu-like symptoms and bird exposure. Seasonal influenza vaccination is recommended for high-risk occupational groups to reduce co-infection risks, though it does not confer protection against zoonotic strains.
Original source text (English)
Home About Clinical Authors Most read this year GP Opinion Videos & Podcasts Professional Comments policy RACGP poll Menu Search for: Advertising newsGP > Clinical Jo Roberts 12 Aug 2026 News Bird flu threat ‘very low’ for humans: CSIRO Jo Roberts 12/08/2026 3:47:12 PM Australia’s H5N1 outbreak is prompting vigilance among authorities, but experts say human transmission risk remains low. The risk of H5N1 transmission from birds to humans ‘remains very low’, says CSIRO experts. Experts from the CSIRO’s Australian Centre for Disease Preparedness (ACDP) say the threat of the H5N1 bird flu spreading to humans remains ‘very low’, following Australia’s first recorded mass bird mortality event last week. It comes as the Federal Government moves to vaccinate all native birds in captivity and Victorian authorities prepare to vaccinate around 5000 little penguins at Phillip Island and St Kilda, in an effort to stem the predicted mass mortalities. H5N1 had been detected in every continent except Australia and greater Oceania until June, when the first Australian case was confirmed in a brown skua seabird in Western Australia. ACDP Director Dr Debbie Eagles said for GPs, most guidance would come from epidemiological information. ‘That might be that people present with flu-like symptoms and report exposure to wild birds that have been sick, potentially ones that have tested positive,’ she said. ‘So, people who have been involved in what might be considered high-risk activities, such as direct contact with sick or wild birds.’ She said the whole genome sequence testing the ACDP conducts ‘can help identify the source of the virus, identify changes as we get more detections, can help characterise local transmission events and look for reassortment with influenza viruses that are already circulating in Australian birds.’ ACDP Senior Research Scientist Dr Frank Wong confirmed there has not been any recorded human cases of the disease in Australia related to this outbreak. He said an ‘ecological perfect storm’ would be required for the virus to spill over, but that did not mitigate the need to stay on high alert. Dr Wong added that most recorded cases in humans have presented as mild respiratory infections and/or conjunctivitis, with just two being more severe illness ‘and they were linked to people who were either elderly or had predisposing conditions’. ‘The risk is very low and remains very low for transmission between birds and people. And that’s what we’ve seen internationally over the last few years with this strain,’ he said. He said since 2024–25, there was a spike in human infections in the United States, the majority linked to infected dairy farms, and two outbreaks in poultry farms. ‘Exposure is with people in professions that expose themselves to outbreak environments,’ Dr Wong said. However, he stressed that they were from a different genotype to the H5N1 virus that is seen in Australia. ‘To date, there’s no clear predictor for the ability of these viruses to jump from birds to mammals, and from birds and mammals to humans,’ Dr Wong said. ‘Even in cases where we’ve found the virus in either mammals or humans, the majority of the viruses that are isolated from these cases, it’s still very much an avian-adapted virus, with the majority avian genetic signatures.’ He said people in high-risk occupations ‘should get vaccinated with the standard flu vaccine’ A recent British study of 428 people working with, or exposed to, birds infected with influenza A (H5N1) clade 2.3.4.4b viruses (primarily poultry sites), found low rates of human infection among participants. Beginning in 2023, the original study cohort included only asymptomatic people exposed to infected birds, while those with respiratory symptoms were recruited from January 2024 onwards. Less than 1% of participants met the study’s criteria for infection, resulting in two confirmed cases and one unclear case. No severe illness was reported; two of the six PCR-positive participants had mild symptoms. Full personal protective equipment was worn by 85% of participants, who self-reported consistent use, with no breaches, while within the biosecurity area. Only 19% reported having received an influenza vaccination. The researchers noted that although seasonal influenza vaccines do not offer protection to humans against zoonotic influenza, ‘they should be considered for those occupationally exposed to zoonotic influenza to reduce the risk of co-infection and thus reassortment events’. Time
Published 12 August 2026; event date referenced as August 2026.
Location and population
Australia (Western Australia, Victoria); affected populations include native birds in captivity, little penguins at Phillip Island and St Kilda (Victoria), and occupationally exposed humans.
Event
Australian health authorities and CSIRO experts assess the risk of H5N1 avian influenza transmission from birds to humans as 'very low' following Australia’s first recorded mass bird mortality event. The event is reported as under investigation with ongoing monitoring.
Scale, impact and risk
No human cases of H5N1 linked to the current Australian outbreak have been recorded. Internationally, most human H5N1 cases have been mild respiratory infections or conjunctivitis; severe cases were linked to elderly individuals or those with predisposing conditions. A British study of 428 occupationally exposed individuals found less than 1% met infection criteria, with two confirmed mild cases and no severe illness. The Federal Government plans vaccination of captive native birds; Victoria plans to vaccinate approximately 5,000 little penguins.
Response, status and uncertainty
CSIRO’s Australian Centre for Disease Preparedness (ACDP) is conducting whole-genome sequencing to track virus origin, detect changes, characterize local transmission, and monitor reassortment with circulating Australian avian influenza viruses. Authorities emphasize vigilance and high alert status despite low transmission risk. GPs are advised to rely on epidemiological information for guidance, particularly for patients with flu-like symptoms and bird exposure.

在接触受感染禽类的工人中,H5N1 感染仍然罕见 - 新闻-医疗

H5N1 infections remain rare among workers exposed to infected birds - News-Medical
新闻简报最近于 2023 年 4 月至 2025 年 3 月在英国进行的一项 队列研究 研究发现,在接触受感染鸟类的工人中,高致病性禽流感 A(H5N1) clade 2.3.4.4b 的人类感染仍然很少见。在 428 名参与者中,只有 2 例确诊病例,1 例情况不明,大多数参与者报告一致使用个人防护装备 (PPE)。研究指出,85% 的参与者使用了个人防护装备,并且没有发现严重疾病或人传人的 传播。研究人员强调了个人防护装备和与受感染鸟类保持足够距离以防止感染的重要性。该研究发表在 Eurosurveillance 上,强调需要继续采取 监测 和更广泛的公共卫生应对措施。研究结果表明,在研究条件下,人畜共患感染和严重疾病的风险较低。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究于2023年4月至2025年3月期间进行,最新数据来自2025年。发表日期为2026-08-11。
地点与对象
该研究重点关注英格兰,特别是 34 个受感染的场所,包括家禽场所和动物救援场所。人口中包括 428 名暴露者,主要是参与扑杀的工人,其中 178 人来自约克郡和亨伯地区。
事件
队列研究 的一项研究发现,在接触受感染鸟类的工人中,人类 H5N1 感染很少见,428 名参与者中只有 2 例确诊病例和 1 例不明病例。研究还指出,大多数参与者都使用了个人防护装备(PPE),并且没有报告严重疾病或人传人的 传播。
规模、影响与风险
在 862 名暴露者中,有 428 人被招募。在没有佩戴个人防护装备的暴露人群中,只有 3% 的甲型流感 (H5) 检测呈阳性,而使用个人防护装备的人群中这一比例为 1%。没有报告严重疾病,也没有发现人传人的 传播。该研究得出的结论是,在研究条件下,甲型流感(H5N1)分支2.3.4.4b病毒导致人畜共患感染和严重疾病的风险较低。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究发表在《欧洲监视》杂志上。研究人员建议继续使用 监测、个人防护装备,并对高危接触者的无症状个体进行检测。研究结果基于 PCR 检测、全 基因组 测序和病毒培养,其中一些病例由于样本日期问题被归类为不清楚。该研究还强调需要采取更广泛的公共卫生应对措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

H5N1 infections remain rare among workers exposed to infected birds - News-Medical

News Brief
A recent cohort study conducted in England between April 2023 and March 2025 found that human infections with highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) clade 2.3.4.4b remained rare among workers exposed to infected birds. Out of 428 participants, only 2 were confirmed cases and 1 was unclear, with most participants reporting consistent use of personal protective equipment (PPE). The study noted that 85% of participants used PPE, and no severe illness or human-to-human transmission was identified. The researchers emphasized the importance of PPE and adequate distance from infected birds to prevent infections. The study was published in Eurosurveillance and highlighted the need for continued surveillance and a broader public health response. The findings suggest a low risk of zoonotic infection and severe disease under the studied conditions.
Original source text (English)
Despite intense exposure at poultry outbreak sites, human H5N1 infections were uncommon and mild, reinforcing the value of PPE while underscoring the need for continued surveillance as the virus evolves. A cohort study of persons exposed to highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) at premises with infected animals, England, 2023 to 2025. Image Credit: Dejan Dundjerski / Shutterstock A recent cohort study found low rates of human infection with highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) clade 2.3.4.4b among workers exposed to infected birds at outbreak premises, with most participants reporting the use of protective equipment. The study was published in the journal Eurosurveillance. Background From 2020 onwards, the number of highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI) A infections among birds has increased, accompanied by corresponding increases in mammalian spillover events. This phenomenon has crossed continents, though the greatest number of cases have been in Europe and the Americas. However, this might reflect limited surveillance and reporting elsewhere, according to the authors. Between 2003 and 2009, there were 468 reported HPAI A(H5N1) cases, with 60% being fatal. In fact, zoonotic A(H5N1) spillover infections have historically been associated with severe illness in humans. In the UK, the 2024/25 avian influenza season saw 81 outbreaks of HPAI A(H5N1). These have often been dealt with by culling and mandatory housing orders to protect public and animal health. Continued circulation and evolution of the virus could increase the risk of mutations that enable human-to-human transmission, a major step towards a pandemic, given the lack of population immunity. Human infection with the current clade 2.3.4.4b was rare until 2024, when the B3.13 genotype emerged in dairy cattle in the USA, followed by the D1.1 genotype. Regular sequencing in the UK has so far found no known reassortment between UK-circulating genotypes and North American strains. There have been no known instances of human-to-human transmission of the 2.3.4.4b clade, but mild infections may still go unrecognized. These could promote viral adaptation to human hosts. Therefore, the World Health Organization (WHO) recommends testing of asymptomatic individuals with high-risk contact with infected poultry or potentially contaminated environments. The current study was conducted between April 2023 and March 2025. It aimed to assess the risk posed by avian influenza to human health, including the occurrence and frequency of such infections in exposed individuals, risk factors, and infection characteristics. Study characteristics The study cohort originally included only asymptomatic individuals exposed to infected birds, while those with respiratory symptoms were recruited from January 2024 onwards. It involved 34 infected premises, with 18 remote and 16 in-person recruitments. At these sites, of the 862 exposed people, 428 were recruited, including 68 recruited at multiple sites. In-person recruitment was more successful than remote recruitment, an important point given that recruitment becomes difficult during an ongoing outbreak. Most of the included sites were poultry sites, and one was an animal rescue site. Of the 428 participants, 178 were from the Yorkshire and Humber regions. The median participant age was 37 years, with 69% male. Most were involved in culling, with half employed by the Animal and Plant Health Agency (APHA) and just under half by contractors. Self-collected nasopharyngeal swabs were tested by polymerase chain reaction (PCR) for the influenza A M gene. Influenza A-positive samples were subtyped for H5, and all samples were tested for influenza B and respiratory syncytial virus (RSV). H5-positive samples underwent confirmatory testing, including whole-genome sequencing and viral culture when possible. Preventive measures The researchers found that 85% of participants reported consistent full personal protective equipment (PPE) use, with no breaches, while within the biosecurity area. During culling, around 98% of APHA and contractor staff, but only 27% of farmers or farm workers, used full PPE. We recommend - Clinical Management of Patients With Relapsed/Refractory Multiple Myeloma Treated With TalquetamabChari A, et al. Clin Lymphoma Myeloma Leuk, 2024. Sponsored by Johnson & Johnson US Medical Affairs - Real-world effectiveness of palbociclib in combination with an aromatase inhibitor in HR+/HER2− bone-only metastatic breast cancerBrought to you by Pfizer Medical Affairs, EM-USA-plb-0207 - First-in-Human, Phase I Study of Sigvotatug Vedotin, an Integrin Beta-6–Directed Antibody-Drug Conjugate: Results From Dose Expansion in Advanced Non–Small Cell...Brought to you by Pfizer Medical Affairs, EM-USA-SVN-0018 - Real-world progression-free survival 2 (PFS2) and tumor response of CDK4/6 inhibitors plus an aromatase inhibitor in patients with HR+/HER2− metastatic breast c...Brought to you by Pfizer Medical Affairs, EM-USA-plb-0206 The uptake of the influenza vaccine was low, at 19%. About 86% of participants were exposed to infected birds within two meters. Separately, 81% touched infected birds or their tissues, and 62% touched bird waste, including litter or eggs. Contractors were most likely to touch birds or their tissues, while farmers or their workers were most likely to touch bird waste. Risk of infection While 96% of participants provided a day 0 sample, this declined steadily to 79% on day 8. Only six participants were PCR-positive for influenza A(H5), but only three met the defined criteria for A(H5N1) infection. Of these, two were classified as confirmed cases, while the third was classified as unclear due to sample date issues. The other three detections were classified as being of "uncertain significance" and were suspected to reflect transient mucosal contamination rather than infection. Thus, fewer than 1% of participants met the study's criteria for infection, resulting in 2 confirmed cases and 1 unclear case. No severe illness was reported; two of the six PCR-positive participants had mild symptoms. Among the six PCR-positive individuals, two did not wear full PPE during exposure. All six had touched infected birds, and four had contact with bird waste. PPE use among PCR-positive participants Overall, 3% of those exposed without PPE were PCR-positive, compared with 1% of those reporting PPE use; however, not all PCR-positive participants met the criteria for confirmed infection. Prophylaxis and infection risk None of the six individuals who tested positive had been vaccinated against the flu or had taken antiviral medication prior to the test. Among participants with negative PCR results, 85% reported full PPE use, 20% had received seasonal influenza vaccination, and 3% were on or had recently taken antiviral medication to prevent infection. Human-to-human transmission risk No cases of human-to-human transmission were identified despite contact tracing of the six PCR-positive individuals. Prevention of HPAI in humans The results underline the importance of PPE use while ensuring an adequate distance from infected birds. The occurrence of PCR positivity in humans despite reported full PPE use suggests potential challenges with correct PPE donning and doffing, unrecognized breaches, or compliance in these environments. The authors also discuss the need for a broader public health response. In this study, the response benefited from avian influenza being a notifiable infection with established communication routes, but this might not be the case for non-notifiable zoonotic influenza. For instance, pigs express receptors for both avian and mammalian influenza viruses, creating opportunities for genetic reassortment during coinfection. Outdoor rearing may also expose pigs to wild birds, thereby increasing the risk of coinfection. Similarly, the authors argue for wider environmental surveillance in the vicinity of infected premises to better detect emerging strains. Seasonal flu shots do not protect against zoonotic influenza, but they could reduce the risk of coinfection with seasonal influenza and, by doing so, lower the opportunities for reassortment. The authors discuss the possibility of seasonal vaccination for occupationally exposed people. Unlike seasonal influenza vaccines, H5-specific vaccines are also being considered for people at occupational risk. The US and some European countries have begun acquiring H5N1 vaccines for human use, with Finland piloting vaccination among occupationally exposed people. Poultry vaccination has also been implemented nationally in France. Limitations Self-reported PPE use and the potential for unrecognized failures in PPE use might have reduced the reliability of the data. The study was smaller than originally planned due to resource constraints. Recruitment was also hampered by the lack of financial security if the participants tested positive and had to go into isolation. Demographic data were not obtained from all participants, limiting the generalizability. However, the largest proportion of contractors lived in the most deprived areas, and contractors had the highest proportion exposed by touch, while the majority of APHA staff lived in the three least deprived quintiles. Conclusions Taken together, the study findings suggested a low risk of zoonotic infection and severe disease due to the influenza A(H5N1) clade 2.3.4.4b viruses in exposed individuals under the conditions studied. However, apparent mucosal contamination occurred in some cases despite self-reported full PPE use. All positives were linked to direct exposure, with no secondary transmission. The use of full PPE is an important protective strategy, but could be impractical given the long working hours and extreme conditions during culling operations, for example. Chemoprophylaxis may offer an additional preventive option in some exposure settings, but further study is needed to establish its utility and acceptability, given its potential side effects and limited evidence. The authors emphasize the need to maintain biosecurity standards at the highest level. This includes communicating risk and control measures to all workers and ensuring compliance. Enhanced surveillance should be resumed following identification of an increased risk of animal-to-human transmission, to help mitigate the risks posed by H5N1 in poultry outbreak settings. - Halford, F., Blomquist, P. B., Bray, N., et al. (2026). A cohort study of persons exposed to highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) at premises with infected animals, England, 2023 to 2025. Eurosurveillance. Time
The study was conducted between April 2023 and March 2025, with the most recent data from 2025. The publication date is 2026-08-11.
Location and population
The study focused on England, specifically on 34 infected premises, including poultry sites and an animal rescue site. The population included 428 exposed individuals, primarily workers involved in culling, with 178 from the Yorkshire and Humber regions.
Event
A cohort study found that human H5N1 infections were rare among workers exposed to infected birds, with only 2 confirmed cases and 1 unclear case out of 428 participants. The study also noted that most participants used personal protective equipment (PPE) and that no severe illness or human-to-human transmission was reported.
Scale, impact and risk
Out of 862 exposed people, 428 were recruited. Only 3% of those exposed without PPE tested positive for influenza A(H5), compared to 1% of those using PPE. No severe illness was reported, and no human-to-human transmission was identified. The study concluded a low risk of zoonotic infection and severe disease due to the influenza A(H5N1) clade 2.3.4.4b viruses under the studied conditions.
Response, status and uncertainty
The study was published in the journal Eurosurveillance. The researchers recommend continued surveillance, PPE use, and testing of asymptomatic individuals with high-risk contact. The findings are based on PCR testing, whole-genome sequencing, and viral culture, with some cases classified as unclear due to sample date issues. The study also highlights the need for a broader public health response.

研究人员开发出潜在的 疫苗 来保护牛免受禽流感 - The Cattle Site

Researchers develop potential vaccine to protect cattle from bird flu - The Cattle Site
新闻简报据 The Cattle Site 2026 年 8 月 10 日报道,研究人员开发了一种潜在的 疫苗 来保护牛免受禽流感的侵害。疫苗 策略旨在针对多种 H5N1 病毒株,旨在减少该疾病对牛群运营和农业系统的影响。这一发展是加强牲畜 监测 和预防措施的更广泛努力的一部分。该报告强调了监测牲畜是否有 禽流感 迹象的重要性,特别是在威尔士和英格兰等病例不断上升的地区。虽然 疫苗 是一项有希望的进步,但没有提供具体的病例数、死亡数或暴露数量。该研究基于爱荷华州立大学进行的研究,该研究强调需要加强对牲畜 禽流感 的监测。该信息是截至发布之日的最新信息,并不表明牛群中存在任何正在进行的 暴发 或确诊病例。

Researchers develop potential vaccine to protect cattle from bird flu - The Cattle Site

News Brief
Researchers have developed a potential vaccine to protect cattle from bird flu, as reported by The Cattle Site on 10 August 2026. The vaccine strategy is designed to target multiple H5N1 strains, aiming to reduce the impact of the disease on cattle operations and agricultural systems. This development is part of broader efforts to enhance livestock surveillance and prevention measures. The report highlights the importance of monitoring livestock for signs of avian influenza, particularly in regions like Wales and England where cases have been rising. While the vaccine is a promising advancement, no specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers are provided. The study is based on research conducted by Iowa State University, which emphasizes the need for increased monitoring of livestock for avian influenza. The information is current as of the publication date and does not indicate any ongoing outbreak or confirmed cases in cattle herds.

Researchers developed a potential vaccine to protect cattle from bird flu, reported by The Cattle Site. This is a research development, not an outbreak or confirmed case.
Scale, impact and risk
The vaccine strategy targets multiple H5N1 strains and could help reduce disease impacts on cattle operations and agricultural systems. No specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers are reported.
Response, status and uncertainty
The development is a research initiative, and no specific investigation, testing, or prevention measures are detailed. The source is a news report, and the information is current as of the publication date.

Australia to roll out bird flu vaccines for most vulnerable native birds - BBC
新闻简报Australia is implementing a bird flu vaccination program for native bird species most vulnerable to the H5N1 strain, following the confirmation of its first case in June 2026 and the first mass mortality event last week. The government has allocated AU$2m for the initiative, with states and territories managing the rollout. Over 230 cases have been confirmed since the initial detection, and some species are at risk of possible extinction while hundreds may face severe population reductions. The risk to humans remains low, with no evidence of impact on poultry or agriculture sectors. Environment Minister Murray Watt described the outbreak as a serious event, noting that more spread and dead birds are expected. The program aims to protect priority species, though the spread among wild birds is anticipated to continue.
查看新闻五要素
时间
澳大利亚于 2026 年 6 月报告了第一例 H5N1 病毒株病例,上周发生了第一起大规模 死亡率 事件。疫苗接种 预计将在未来几天或几周内开始推出。
地点与对象
澳大利亚,特别关注本土鸟类和圈养鸟类,在南澳大利亚海岸附近的一个岛屿上观察到首次大规模 死亡率 事件。
事件
继 2026 年 6 月确认首例病例以及上周首次大规模 死亡率 事件之后,澳大利亚正在针对最易感染 H5N1 病毒株的本地鸟类推出禽流感 疫苗。这是根据政府官方声明确认的事件。
规模、影响与风险
自6月份出现第一例病例以来,已确诊超过230例。政府确定了少数物种可能面临灭绝,数百种物种面临人口严重减少的风险。对人类的风险仍然很低,没有证据表明对家禽或农业部门有影响。
应对、状态与不确定性
The government has allocated AU$2m for vaccinations, with states and territories managing the rollout. The program targets priority species most vulnerable to bird flu, and the outbreak is described as a serious event. The risk to humans remains low, and the spread among wild birds is expected to continue.

Australia to roll out bird flu vaccines for most vulnerable native birds - BBC

News Brief
Australia is implementing a bird flu vaccination program for native bird species most vulnerable to the H5N1 strain, following the confirmation of its first case in June 2026 and the first mass mortality event last week. The government has allocated AU$2m for the initiative, with states and territories managing the rollout. Over 230 cases have been confirmed since the initial detection, and some species are at risk of possible extinction while hundreds may face severe population reductions. The risk to humans remains low, with no evidence of impact on poultry or agriculture sectors. The program aims to protect priority species, though the spread among wild birds is anticipated to continue.
Original source text (English)
Australia to roll out bird flu vaccines for most vulnerable native birds 3 days ago Lana Lam Sydney Getty Images Australia was the last continent to report cases of the highly contagious H5N1 strain Australia will roll out bird flu vaccines in the "coming days and weeks" with native species and birds in captivity first in line for protection from the deadly disease. On Tuesday, the government said that it had AU$2m ($1.4m, £1m) worth of vaccinations for the H5N1 strain - about 1,500 vials - with states and territories to manage the rollout. Australia reported its first case of the highly contagious strain in June, making it the last continent to be impacted. Since then, more than 230 cases have been confirmed. Last week, it saw its first mass mortality from bird flu, with about 50 greater crested terns killed by the disease on an island off the coast of South Australia. Agriculture Minister Julie Collins said that while it could not stop the spread of outbreak as it worsened, the programme would target "priority species" most vulnerable to bird flu. The risk to humans remained low, Collins said, and there was no evidence that the disease had impacted the poultry or agriculture sectors. She said while little could be done to stop the spread among wild birds and across the country, "we can take action to protect the species most at risk". "The first vaccines are expected to roll out in coming days and weeks based on an agreed national approach to the targeted vaccination of priority native bird species," she said. "Australians should expect to see more spread and larger numbers, particularly of dead birds and mass mortalities around the country from the H5 bird flu." Environment Minister Murray Watt said birds would each need two doses and that the outbreak was a "serious event". Earlier this month, the government released a list assessing the risk level of bird flu for more than 2,000 of the country's unique native birds and mammals, based on their vulnerability and susceptibility. A handful of species were identified as facing possible extinction while hundreds of others may suffer a "severe reduction" in populations. Australia confirms first case of H5N1 bird flu as virus reaches every continent Australia

南澳大利亚内陆发现禽流感,鸟类饲养员呼吁采取 疫苗

Bird keepers call for vaccines as bird flu detected inland in South Australia
新闻简报南澳内陆地区已检测到禽流感(H5N1),距罗布100多公里,这里一直是澳大利亚禽流感暴发的焦点。包括 Louise 和 Al McKay 在内的野生动物专家和鸟类饲养员正在敦促使用 疫苗 来保护濒危物种和私人鸟类。在石灰岩海岸和塔皮纳等内陆地区的凤头燕鸥中发现了这种病毒。南澳大利亚州政府正在制定一项国家战略,将 疫苗接种 用于优先圈养物种,例如仅在袋鼠岛上发现的极度濒危光泽黑色凤头鹦鹉。政府发言人指出,由于需要多次剂量,向自由生活的物种提供 疫苗 很困难。麦凯夫妇已实施生物安全措施,包括修改围栏和监测 野生鸟类,并正在等待案件确认。他们强调采取积极措施保护鸟类的重要性,因为它们很难重新安置并且需要特定的条件。该病毒正在大范围传播,鼓励社区报告潜在病例并对病禽保持警惕。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 8 月 14 日星期五上午 11:10。
地点与对象
南澳大利亚州,特别是距罗布内陆 100 公里的佩诺拉 (Penola) 地区,以及包括石灰石海岸、麦克唐奈港、绍森德和菲利普岛海豹岩在内的沿海地区。

南澳大利亚内陆地区发现了禽流感(H5N1),距离罗布100多公里处报告了新病例,罗布一直是澳大利亚禽流感的焦点地区暴发。野生动物专家和鸟类饲养者呼吁 疫苗 保护濒危物种和私人鸟类。

在石灰岩海岸和塔皮纳等内陆地区的凤头燕鸥中发现了这种病毒。南澳大利亚州政府正致力于保护圈养的优先物种,包括仅在袋鼠岛上发现的极度濒危的光泽黑色凤头鹦鹉。据描述,这种风险持续存在并在大片地区蔓延。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bird keepers call for vaccines as bird flu detected inland in South Australia

News Brief
In South Australia, bird flu (H5N1) has been detected inland, more than 100 kilometres from Robe, which has been a focal point of Australia's bird flu outbreak. Wildlife experts and bird keepers, including Louise and Al McKay, are urging the availability of vaccines to protect at-risk species and privately owned birds. The virus has been found in crested terns along the Limestone Coast and in inland areas like Tarpeena. The South Australian government is part of a national strategy to use vaccinations for priority captive species, such as the critically endangered glossy black cockatoo found only on Kangaroo Island. The government spokesperson noted the difficulty in delivering vaccines to free-living species due to the need for multiple doses. The McKays have implemented biosecurity measures, including modifying enclosures and monitoring for wild birds, and are awaiting confirmation of the case. They emphasize the importance of proactive measures to safeguard their birds, which are difficult to rehouse and require specific conditions. The virus is spreading across large regions, and the community is encouraged to report potential cases and remain vigilant around sick birds.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content Skip to news navigation, settings and search Explore the ABC More from ABC Close menuABC ABC News ABC iview ABC listen Kids & Students Indigenous Information & Services news iview listen Loading ABC News Just In For You Politics World Business Analysis Sport Lifestyle Entertainment Quizzes More Search News Home Just In For You Analysis Rural Watch Live Health Indigenous Politics Science Deep Time Elections World Environment Investigations Business Local news Quizzes Sport AFL NRL Football Tennis Cricket Netball Lifestyle Wellbeing Relationships & Family Food & Recipes Personal Finance Home & Garden Entertainment TV & Movies Books Music Pop Culture Arts ABC News News Home Bird keepers call for vaccines as bird flu detected inland in South Australia ShareShare article Bird keepers call for vaccines as bird flu detected inland in South Australia By Liz Rymill ABC South East SA Topic:Avian Influenza Posted Fri 14 Aug 2026 at 11:10amFri 14 Aug 2026 at 11:10amFri 14 Aug 2026 at 11:10am Louise McKay is worried about her birds, including her masked owl. (ABC South East SA: Liz Rymill) In short: Backyard bird keepers are calling for vaccines to be made available to protect at-risk species and privately owned birds as avian influenza spreads inland. There have been reports of new cases more than 100 kilometres inland from Robe in South Australia, which has been a focal point of Australia's bird flu outbreak. What's next? The South Australian government says it is part of a national approach using vaccinations to protect some of the most vulnerable native birds. Wildlife experts are calling for vaccines to be made available to backyard bird keepers to protect flocks from avian influenza as the deadly virus spreads inland in some parts of Australia. Wildlife educators Al and Louise McKay, who live 1 kilometre from a virus detection site in regional South Australia, said they were on high alert and worried about their rare birds. The couple lives in the Penola district, about 100 kilometres inland from the tourist town of Robe, which has been a focal point of Australia's bird flu outbreak. Louise and Al McKay are calling for vaccines to be available to backyard bird keepers. (ABC South East SA: Liz Rymill) They are looking at the potential of vaccinating two main species kept under licence: the glossy black cockatoo and the masked owl. In South Australia, this particular species of glossy black cockatoo is critically endangered and only found on Kangaroo Island. "We're very concerned," Mr McKay said. "We want to be proactive and not caught on the back foot. "Our birds are difficult to rehouse and need certain conditions, including light, so vaccinating would be a more practical measure." Louise McKay has modified enclosures to expand biosecurity measures. (ABC South East SA: Liz Rymill) The couple, who run an educational wildlife business that exhibits at aged care homes, schools, country shows and public events, said they would also look at changing their exhibitions and keeping their birds off the program until vaccinations were available. Mrs McKay said they had implemented biosecurity measures, including moving water bowls so they could not be accessed by wild birds, and checking regularly for droppings and for wild birds landing on enclosures. "A lot of bird keepers are anxious about this evolving situation," Mrs McKay said. "Making vaccines available would alleviate some of the stress and provide some assurance our treasured animals are safe." Cases spike along coast H5N1 bird flu has been detected in crested terns along the Limestone Coast, from Robe to Port MacDonnell and Southend. Multiple dead birds can be seen lying on beaches and foreshores at these coastal towns. A dead seagull was found near the Robe boat ramp last week. (ABC South East SA: Josh Brine) "We've never seen a crested tern in our area, but I'm told they fly to Lake Mundi and sometimes on the Glenelg River in Victoria," Mr McKay said. Another greater crested tern was also found this week inland at Tarpeena, while across the border in Victoria, a further seven cases of H5N1 bird flu have been detected in greater crested terns in Phillip Island, Mildura, Warrnambool, Hamilton, Hawkesdale and St Andrews Beach. Agriculture Victoria chief biodiversity officer James Todd said the detections in new parts of the state, including at Seal Rocks on Phillip Island, were significant developments. "These detections show us that the virus is continuing to move through wild bird populations, and its ability to move across large regions," he said. A South Australian government spokesperson this week said South Australia was part of a national approach using vaccinations to protect some of the most vulnerable native birds. The spokesperson said the focus would be on "priority species in captivity". They said it was "difficult" and "impractical" to deliver vaccines to most "free-living species" because many vaccines required multiple doses. "Vaccination is another tool which we add to our range of preparedness strategies including surveillance, biosecurity measures and conservation actions to reduce other threats and disturbance — to help protect our wildlife," the spokesperson said. Community has role to play Mrs McKay encouraged community members to continue reporting and recording potential avian influenza cases and to be vigilant around sick birds. She said the infected bird they reported near their home "did attempt to flutter but couldn't get any elevation, was walking in circles, looking down, and wasn't able to move any great distance". Greater crested terns are some of the first birds to have contracted the H5N1 bird flu virus in Australia. (iNaturalist: Tommy Osborne, Greater crested tern, CC BY-NC 4.0) "When I saw this tern so close by, I went into a panic that it had to be collected quickly because I'd have hated any wildlife to start scavenging on it and then land on our aviaries at home," she said. "That side of things really worries me." Louise McKay wants to protect her endangered glossy black cockatoo. (ABC South East SA: Liz Rymill) The couple said they had been satisfied with the reporting process, and were awaiting a follow-up call to confirm the case. "We took an extra step and contacted one of the senior ranger officers who assured us it would be dealt with quickly," Mrs McKay said. "The next time we approached the area, the bird had been removed." Native birds to be vaccinated against H5N1 bird flu in Australia - independent.co.uk
新闻简报澳大利亚将在几天内开始为本地鸟类接种 H5N1 禽流感 疫苗,在该国首次报告与该病毒相关的大规模海鸟死亡事件后,将优先考虑圈养的脆弱物种。该倡议由农业部长朱莉·柯林斯 (Julie Collins) 宣布,目标是被视为高风险的物种,澳大利亚 850 种鸟类中约 45% 是特有且特别脆弱的物种。该计划与新西兰 7 月份为保护 300 只濒临灭绝的繁殖鸟类而发起的类似行动相呼应。虽然范围强调了对 H5N1 从候鸟传播到当地野生动物的高度关注,但该公告没有详细说明测试方案、隔离措施或更广泛的野生动物 监测 策略。
查看新闻五要素

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Native birds to be vaccinated against H5N1 bird flu in Australia - independent.co.uk

News Brief
Australia will begin vaccinating native bird populations against H5N1 avian influenza within days, prioritizing vulnerable species in captivity following the country’s first reported mass seabird deaths linked to the virus. The initiative, announced by Agriculture Minister Julie Collins, targets species deemed at high risk, with roughly 45% of Australia’s 850 bird species endemic and uniquely vulnerable. The program mirrors a similar effort launched by New Zealand in July to protect 300 endangered breeding birds. While the scope underscores heightened concern over H5N1’s spread from migratory birds to local wildlife, the announcement does not detail testing protocols, isolation measures, or broader wildlife surveillance strategies.
Original source text (English)

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

澳大利亚:野生鸟类 禽流感检测量在不到 4 天内翻倍 - 禽流感日记

Australia: Bird Flu Detection in Wild Birds Doubles in Less Than 4 Days - Avian Flu Diary
新闻简报2026 年 8 月 12 日,澳大利亚农业、渔业和林业部将其 H5 禽流感 报告从个体鸟类检测转向汇总“事件”,以应对野生鸟类病例激增的情况。截至 8 月 13 日,六个州和地区记录了 186 起 H5 事件,其中塔斯马尼亚州报告首次在野生棕色贼鸥身上发现本地阳性病例。目前还没有发现家禽或牲畜的报告,但当局警告称,入侵敏感家禽区的可能性越来越大。塔斯马尼亚正在扩大野生动物监测,并在国家AUSVETPLAN框架下准备应对措施,同时CSIRO进行验证性测试。疫苗 仍然不适用于澳大利亚的大多数野生物种和家禽。
查看新闻五要素
时间
活动日期:2026年8月12日至13日(报告窗口)。出版日期:2026 年 8 月 8 日(禽流感日记发布日期)。
地点与对象
澳大利亚(全国),西澳大利亚州(10 起)、南澳大利亚州(123 起)、新南威尔士州(4 起)、昆士兰州(1 起)、维多利亚州(48 起)和塔斯马尼亚州(首次在当地发现野生棕色贼鸥)已确诊事件。
事件
自 2026 年 8 月 12 日起,澳大利亚农业、渔业和林业部 (DAFF) 将 野生鸟类 中 H5 禽流感 的报告方法从个体检测改为汇总“事件”。塔斯马尼亚于 2026 年 8 月 13 日报告了首例本地 H5 阳性 野生鸟类(棕色贼鸥)。
规模、影响与风险
As of 13 August 2026, 186 confirmed H5 bird flu events in wild birds nationwide. No detections in poultry, livestock, or non-avian wildlife reported. Risk assessment notes rapid expansion of H5 detections in wild birds and potential future incursions into sensitive poultry zones.
应对、状态与不确定性
DAFF 现在报告汇总的“事件”而不是单独的检测来管理大量案件;塔斯马尼亚州在检测区域周围扩大了 监测,并正在根据 AUSVETPLAN 制定应对计划。澳大利亚没有批准用于家禽或大多数野生物种的 疫苗;澳大利亚联邦科学与工业研究组织 (CSIRO) 正在对塔斯马尼亚样本进行确认和分型。数据反映州/地区向 DAFF 提交的资料;不同司法管辖区可能会出现差异。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Australia: Bird Flu Detection in Wild Birds Doubles in Less Than 4 Days - Avian Flu Diary

News Brief
On 12 August 2026, Australia’s Department of Agriculture, Fisheries and Forestry shifted its H5 avian influenza reporting from individual bird detections to aggregated ‘events’ to manage a surge in wild-bird cases. By 13 August, 186 confirmed H5 events were recorded across six states and territories, with Tasmania reporting its first local positive in a wild brown skua. No poultry or livestock detections have been reported, but authorities warn that incursions into sensitive poultry zones are increasingly likely. Tasmania is expanding wildlife surveillance and preparing response measures under the national AUSVETPLAN framework, while CSIRO conducts confirmatory testing. Vaccines remain unavailable for most wild species and poultry in Australia.
Original source text (English)
Avian Flu Diary Covering Pandemic and Seasonal Flu, Emerging Infectious Diseases, public health, community & Individual preparedness. NOTE: All AFD blogs are written by a human - any mistakes are solely mine. Thursday, August 13, 2026 Australia Changes How They Report H5N1 - Tasmania Detects 1st H5 Positive Bird Credit https://www.agriculture.gov.au/campaigns/birdflu #19,291 As we discussed a month ago, the number of daily citizen reports of sick or dead birds in Australia was already far outpacing their government's ability to investigate and test carcasses. Graphic generated by Gemini Yesterday, DAFF announced major changes in how they will report H5N1. No longer will individual detections be announced, instead they will track individual `events'. Event reporting *From 12 August 2026, we have changed how we report on H5 bird flu detections. Reporting will be based on events, rather than individual bird detections, where an event may involve one or more birds. The new number of positive events captures all previously reported individual confirmed detections. As of 4pm AEST, 13 August 2026, Australia has 186 confirmed events of H5 bird flu in wild birds. 10 in Western Australia (WA) 123 in South Australia (SA) 4 in New South Wales (NSW) 1 in Queensland (QLD) 48 in Victoria (VIC). As H5 bird flu is confirmed in more locations and species in Australia it will not be necessary to continue testing all species in known areas of transmission, or to test every bird involved in an investigation. Reporting will be targeted to provide a clear picture of the national H5 bird flu situation in wildlife in Australia and key developments. Definitions Investigation: An event where one or more wild birds or wild mammals showing signs of H5 bird flu have been reported to state or territory authorities and samples have been collected for laboratory testing. Positive event: An investigation where H5 bird flu has been confirmed through laboratory testing. Negative event: An investigation where laboratory testing has ruled out H5 bird flu. Hotline report: A report to relevant state or territory authorities through the Emergency Animal Disease Hotline or other official sources. Disclaimer Data reflects information provided by state and territory governments to the Australian Government as at 17:00 AEST daily. The Australian Government publishes this information for national reporting purposes. Responsibility for the accuracy, completeness and currency of the data remains with the relevant state or territory government. Due to differences in reporting timing, information on the national dashboard may differ from information published on state or territory government websites. While it is always disappointing to get less precise data, with the number of citizen reports (n=18,869) now running 13 times higher than the number of completed investigations (n=1459), something had to give. It isn't clear how this change will affect individual state and territorial reporting, as this new format hasn't yet appeared on either the Victoria or South Australia bird flu update web pages. Meanwhile, Tasmania has reported its first local positive detection of H5 in a brown skua. Samples have been sent to CSIRO for confirmation and subtyping. This from NRE Tas. H5 bird flu detected in Tasmania The Department of Natural Resources and Environment Tasmania (NRE Tas) has detected H5 avian influenza (bird flu) in a wild brown skua. The bird was identified through NRE Tas surveillance efforts near Rocky Cape in the state’s north west and sent for testing at the Animal Health Laboratory in Launceston. Following the initial positive test result, samples have been sent to the CSIRO’s Australian Centre for Disease Preparedness in Geelong to confirm the positive detection. This is Tasmania’s first positive detection in a wild bird. There have been no detections in poultry and there is no evidence of other species mortalities at this time. Tasmania has been preparing for the arrival of H5 bird flu, working with other government agencies, stakeholders and the community, and is well prepared to respond. NRE Tas will expand planned surveillance in and around the detection area, including King and Flinders islands. This will include further wildlife monitoring, sample collection and risk assessment activities. Tasmania has a robust biosecurity system but it is not possible to stop infected wildlife from entering Tasmania. The Tasmanian Avian Influenza Readiness and Response Plan sets out how NRE Tas will respond to an outbreak of H5 bird flu, and how you can prepare. In the event of an outbreak, our response will also be guided by the Animal Health Australia AUSVETPLAN response strategy for avian influenza. AUSVETPLAN contains the nationally agreed approach for responding to emergency animal disease incidents in Australia. Tasmania is also actively advancing vaccination for captive Orange-bellied Parrots as an additional protection measure for this endangered species however vaccination is not practical for most wild bird populations. There is no approved vaccine in Australia for backyard, domestic or commercial poultry and birds. There is also no vaccine available for seals, dolphins and Tasmanian devils. The Australian Government is working to make new vaccines available and ensure they are safe for use. For now, H5N1 has not been detected in poultry, livestock, or non-avian wildlife. But the odds that status quo will last are slim. Hopefully Australia is working on new and better ways to effectively communicate this rapidly evolving crisis to its public, and the world. A little over 2 weeks ago we looked at a preprint by Raina MacIntyre et al. (HPAI H5N1 risk in Australia: a model for the prediction of poultry outbreaks) which included a map showing current HPAI H5 detections overlaying poultry production zones. You can still view an updated interactive map at EPIWATCH, which shows the continued encroachment of HPAI in wild birds to these sensitive regions. Wednesday, August 12, 2026 J. Virology: Risk assessment of avian influenza A(H5N5) virus from the first human case using the ferret model Credit: Multiple transatlantic incursions of HPAI clade 2.3.4.4b A(H5N5) virus into North America and spillover to mammals #19,290 Last November we saw the first, and as far as we know - only - human infection with emerging H5N5 subtype of avian flu in a resident of Washington State who had contact with backyard poultry and/or wild birds. We've been following HPAI H5N5's spread in birds and small mammals - first in Europe, then in Canada and the United States - for several years, including a report last month; Preprint: HPAI H5N5 in a Polar Bear and Atlantic Walrus, Svalbard, 2026, with Widespread Seroconversion in Polar Bears. In late July we saw a Preprint: A lethal human H5N5 influenza virus isolate exhibits low pandemic risk traits, which somewhat reassuringly found the virus displayed relatively low pandemic risk traits: It replicated relatively poorly (compared to seasonal H1N1) in upper respiratory cell cultures, It has a more avian-like pH inactivation point (about 5.5) compared to human-adapted flu strains And - unlike in the Erdelyan et al. study (which reported rapid 100% mortality and some transmission in ferrets) - they reported that infected ferrets did not infect healthy co-housed ferrets. Today we've a study published in the Journal of Virology by researchers at the CDC's Influenza Division, NCIRD, which is slightly less reassuring. While they still found no evidence of airborne transmission in the ferret model, they report limited direct-contact transmission in 2 of 3 co-housed ferrets. And while the preprint reported moderate disease severity in ferrets, today's study reports uniformly severe/fatal disease, with rapid systemic spread. And where the preprint characterized WA/2148 as having reduced replication in human bronchial epithelial cultures, the CDC study instead found robust replication in polarized Calu-3 airway cells at both 33°C and 37°C. There were differences in both the methods and materials used in these two studies, which could account for these disparities. Today's study even notes that primary human airway cultures or organoids would provide a more physiologically relevant comparison than the proxies used by these two studies. And as always, the caveat remains that we can only draw limited conclusions from the evaluation and characterization of a single isolate, since even minor differences between strains or genotypes can make significant changes to its behavior. Due to it length, and technical nature, I've only reproduced the abstract and some excerpts from the study below. Follow the link to read it in its entirety. I'll have a brief postscript after you return. Risk assessment of avian influenza A(H5N5) virus from the first human case using the ferret model Authors: Joanna Pulit-Penaloza , Jessica A. Belser, Nicole Brock, Troy J. Kieran, Claudia Pappas, Hui Zeng, Xiangjie Sun, Juan A. De La Cruz, Yasuko Hatta, Han Di, C. Todd Davis , Taronna R. Maines https://doi.org/10.1128/jvi.00856-26 PDF/EPUB ABSTRACT The incursion of Eurasian-origin genotype A6 A(H5N5) virus into North America expanded the genetic diversity among North American highly pathogenic avian influenza viruses and heightened concern about zoonotic risk. Following a fatal human infection with the A(H5N5) virus A/Washington/2148/2025, viral replication was assessed in polarized human bronchial epithelial cells, and pathogenicity, transmissibility in direct contact and respiratory droplet models, and airborne virus shedding were evaluated in ferrets to inform pandemic risk assessment. A(H5N5) displayed robust replication in Calu-3 cells at 33°C and 37°C, showing kinetics and peak titers comparable to those of contemporary genotype B3.13 and D1.1 A(H5N1) viruses. In ferrets, A(H5N5) replicated efficiently in the respiratory tract, disseminated to extrapulmonary tissues, and caused fatal disease in all inoculated animals. Airborne transmission was not observed, and infrequent, low-level detection of virus in air samples paralleled that of A(H5) viruses that are not transmissible via air in ferrets. In a direct contact model, limited transmission was detected within 4 days of exposure, with evidence of lower respiratory tract replication in contact animals. These findings indicate that the A(H5N5) virus has the capacity for robust replication in an airway epithelial cell line and can cause severe systemic infection and mortality in ferrets but has not acquired adaptations for airborne spread in mammals. Collectively, these results underscore heterogeneity among clade 2.3.4.4b A(H5Nx) viruses in North America and the need for genotype-by-genotype evaluation of newly emerged viruses to understand public health risk. IMPORTANCE The emergence of Eurasian-origin genotype A6 highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N5) virus in North America has increased viral diversity and raised concerns about zoonotic and pandemic risk. In this study, we evaluated the replication kinetics, pathogenesis, and transmission of A/Washington/2148/2025 A(H5N5) virus, which was isolated from the first reported human infection with this influenza virus subtype, using polarized human bronchial epithelial cells and the ferret model. The A(H5N5) virus replicated efficiently in vitro at temperatures representative of the upper and lower respiratory tracts and caused fatal systemic disease in inoculated ferrets. Limited transmission was observed during 4 days of direct contact. Airborne virus detection was infrequent and did not result in airborne transmission. These findings show that A(H5N5) virus can replicate robustly in mammalian cells and cause severe disease but lacks adaptations supporting efficient airborne spread, informing assessment of the pandemic risk posed by genotype A6 influenza viruses. (SNIP) DISCUSSION The rapid expansion of clade 2.3.4.4b A(H5N1) influenza viruses into novel mammalian hosts has necessitated comprehensive risk assessments of representative viral isolates from different genotypes associated with human infection and detections in mammalian hosts (19, 20). Collectively, these studies have revealed that clade 2.3.4.4b A(H5N1) viruses can replicate efficiently in a diversity of mammalian epithelial cell types derived from the human respiratory tract (21–23), cause severe and fatal disease in animal models, and have varied capacity for airborne transmission to susceptible contacts (4–6, 18, 24). The detection of A(H5N5) virus associated with fatal human disease in 2025 underscores the confounding unpredictability of A(H5) viruses, and their continued threat to human health. (SNIP) Overall, the WA/2148 A(H5N5) virus showed the capacity to cause severe, systemic mammalian infection and robust replication in polarized human airway epithelial cells, characteristics similar to those of other contemporary A(H5Nx) viruses. However, the virus lacked clear adaptation for efficient airborne spread, indicating limited adaptation to a mammalian host. Our findings underscore substantial heterogeneity in pathogenesis and transmission among clade 2.3.4.4b A(H5Nx) viruses, supporting continued genotype-by-genotype evaluations. (Continue . . .) As a pandemic virus, H5N5 may not be ready for prime time, but as a zoonotic threat it already appears to be capable of robust replication in human airway cells and causing severe systemic disease in mammals. All of which makes it a virus very much worth our continued attention. Kent County MI Health Department Bulletin on Swine Flu (in pigs) at Local Fair Credit Wikipedia #19,289 We've seen an unusually quiet year of reporting on Swine Variant Influenza, with only 2 H1N2v (human) cases reported since October 1st of 2025; a far cry from the average of 36 human infections per year since tracking began in 2011 (n=581). The CDC describes swine influenza viruses as: Swine influenza is a respiratory disease of pigs caused by type A influenza viruses that regularly cause outbreaks of influenza in pigs. Influenza viruses that commonly circulate in swine are called “swine influenza viruses” or “swine flu viruses.” Like human influenza viruses, there are different subtypes and strains of swine influenza viruses. The main swine influenza viruses circulating in U.S. pigs in recent years have been, swine triple reassortant (tr) H1N1 influenza virus, trH3N2 virus, and trH1N2 virus. When a swine influenza virus spills over into a human, it is called a swine-variant virus. The CDC's IRAT (Influenza Risk Assessment Tool) lists 3 North American swine viruses as having at least some pandemic potential (2 added in 2019). H1N2 variant [A/California/62/2018] Jul 2019 5.8 5.7 Moderate H3N2 variant [A/Ohio/13/2017] Jul 2019 6.6 5.8 Moderate H3N2 variant [A/Indiana/08/2011] Dec 2012 6.0 4.5 Moderate While no additional cases have been confirmed, this week the Kent County Health Department announced the closure of their county fair's swine barn after the sudden death of a pig, and `several other pigs developed signs of illness'. KCHD is reportedly investigating reports of `influenza-like' illness among individuals who had contact with pigs at the fair, but no confirmed human infections have been reported at this time. They are urging the public: “Anyone who develops flu-like symptoms after having contact with pigs at the fair should contact the Kent County Health Department and let their health care provider know about their exposure.” So far, we've no indication of the subtype involved or the severity of the human `influenza-like' illnesses they are investigating. Typically, swine variant infections are mild or moderate in humans, with only a couple of deaths reported since 2011. The bulletin advises that the Kent County Fair has ended, but attendees who had contact with pigs at the fair should monitor for flu-like symptoms for 10 days. They then go on to list the precautions other fair-goers in Michigan should take when attending agricultural events. First, the Health Department announcement, after which I'll have a bit more. Health Officials Raise Awareness Following Swine Flu Detection in Pigs at the Kent County Youth Fair GRAND RAPIDS, MI. (August 10, 2026) — The Kent County Health Department (KCHD), in partnership with the Michigan Department of Health and Human Services (MDHHS) and the Michigan Department of Agriculture and Rural Development (MDARD), is advising people who may have had contact with pigs at the Kent County Youth Fair to monitor for influenza-like symptoms due to a detection of swine influenza. On Friday, August 7, a pig at the Kent County Youth Fair died suddenly, and several other pigs developed signs of illness. The sick pigs were isolated, and the swine barn was immediately closed to the public. However, as a precaution, the KCHD is investigating reports of influenza-like illness among individuals who had contact with pigs at the fair. “While we do not have confirmation of human infection at this time, we are taking this situation seriously and want people who may have been exposed to know what symptoms to watch for and what to do next,” said Kent County Health Department Health Officer Dr. Adam London. “Anyone who develops flu-like symptoms after having contact with pigs at the fair should contact the Kent County Health Department and let their health care provider know about their exposure.” Signs and Symptoms in Humans Symptoms of swine flu can be similar to the seasonal flu and may include: Fever Lethargy (tiredness) Lack of appetite Coughing Runny nose Sore throat Eye irritation Nausea Vomiting Diarrhea Anyone who had contact with pigs at the fair should monitor for these symptoms for 10 days. Those who develop symptoms should contact their healthcare provider or their local health department for testing. The KCHD Communicable Disease Unit can be reached at 616-632-7228. View five news elements
Time
Event date: 12–13 August 2026 (reporting window). Publication date: 8 August 2026 (Avian Flu Diary post date).
Location and population
Australia (national), with confirmed events in Western Australia (10), South Australia (123), New South Wales (4), Queensland (1), Victoria (48), and Tasmania (first local detection in a wild brown skua).
Event
Australia’s Department of Agriculture, Fisheries and Forestry (DAFF) changed its reporting method for H5 avian influenza in wild birds from individual detections to aggregated ‘events’ starting 12 August 2026. Tasmania reported its first local H5-positive wild bird (brown skua) on 13 August 2026.
Scale, impact and risk
As of 13 August 2026, 186 confirmed H5 bird flu events in wild birds nationwide. No detections in poultry, livestock, or non-avian wildlife reported. Risk assessment notes rapid expansion of H5 detections in wild birds and potential future incursions into sensitive poultry zones.
Response, status and uncertainty
DAFF now reports aggregated ‘events’ rather than individual detections to manage high caseload; Tasmania expanded surveillance around the detection area and is preparing response plans under AUSVETPLAN. No approved vaccines exist for poultry or most wild species in Australia; CSIRO confirmation and subtyping of the Tasmanian sample are pending. Data reflect state/territory submissions to DAFF; discrepancies may occur across jurisdictions.

Tasmania confirms first case of deadly bird flu strain H5N1 - ABC News & Headlines – Australian Broadcasting Corporation
新闻简报据 2026 年 8 月 12 日报道,塔斯马尼亚州确认了首例家禽 高致病性禽流感 A(H5N1) 病例。此次检测标志着该州首次发现 H5N1,并引起了当地家禽行业的担忧,农民被描述为“非常紧张”,原定的家禽展也被取消。当局正在调查 暴发 的范围,该区域已在塔斯马尼亚的第二个地点被发现。现有证据中尚未报告人类感染。该事件凸显了澳大利亚家禽业生物安全风险的增加以及正在进行的 监测 努力。

应对、状态与不确定性

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Tasmania confirms first case of deadly bird flu strain H5N1 - ABC News & Headlines – Australian Broadcasting Corporation

News Brief
Tasmania has confirmed its first case of highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) in poultry, according to ABC News on 12 August 2026. The detection marks the state’s initial H5N1 finding and has prompted concern within the local poultry sector, with farmers described as 'very nervous' and a scheduled poultry show cancelled. Authorities are investigating the extent of the outbreak, which has been detected in a second location in Tasmania. No human infections have been reported in the available evidence. The event underscores heightened biosecurity risks for Australia’s poultry industry and ongoing surveillance efforts.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

柬埔寨确认人类 H5N1 禽流感病例,因为 H5N1 袭击了更多犹他州鸡蛋农场 - CIDRAP

Cambodia confirms human H5N1 avian flu case as H5N1 hits more Utah egg farms - CIDRAP
新闻简报Cambodia confirmed its fifth human H5N1 avian influenza case of 2026 on July 16, involving a 9-month-old girl who was hospitalized in intensive care; the source of infection remains unidentified and close contacts are receiving antiviral prophylaxis. In the United States, the USDA reported a second consecutive week of H5N1 outbreaks in Utah’s poultry sector, affecting 275,100 birds on an egg farm, while 1.49 million birds across the country have been impacted over the past 30 days. Utah and Idaho also reported new H5N1 detections in cattle, bringing the 2026 total to 81 confirmed cases, including 26 detected in the last month. The concurrent spread in animals raises concerns about zoonotic spillover risk, though no direct epidemiological link between the Cambodian human case and the US animal outbreaks has been established.
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-08-12;事件报告时间为 2026 年 7 月 16 日。
地点与对象
柬埔寨(国民);美国犹他州卡什县(地区);受影响的人群包括柬埔寨的一名 9 个月大的女孩、犹他州一个蛋场的家禽以及犹他州和爱达荷州的牛。
事件
柬埔寨卫生部报告了一名 9 个月大女孩确诊的人类 H5N1 禽流感 病例;美国农业部 APHIS 报告在犹他州和爱达荷州的家禽和牛中检测到 H5N1。
规模、影响与风险
Cambodia: 1 confirmed human H5N1 case in 2026; 19 human H5N1 cases in 2025 with 8 deaths. Utah: 275,100 birds affected on an egg farm; 1.49 million birds impacted in the US over the past 30 days. Utah and Idaho: 81 cattle confirmed H5N1 cases in 2026, including 26 in the past 30 days.
应对、状态与不确定性
柬埔寨:儿童住院接受重症监护;感染源正在调查中;密切接触者接受奥司他韦作为预防措施。美国:APHIS 报告家禽和牛正在进行 暴发;证据中没有提供有关控制措施的进一步细节。

Topics & Projects Antimicrobial Stewardship Chronic Wasting Disease Osterholm Update Podcasts Newsletters About CIDRAP CIDRAP in the News Our Staff Our Director Osterholm in the Press Shop Merchandise Contact Us About Search Support Menu Cambodia confirms human H5N1 avian flu case as H5N1 hits more Utah egg farms News brief July 16, 2026 Stephanie Soucheray, MA Topics Avian Influenza (Bird Flu) Share mswine / Flickr cc Earlier this week Cambodian officials announced the country’s fifth human H5N1 avian flu case this year, this one involving a 9-month-old girl. The girl has been hospitalized and is receiving intensive care. So far no source of infection or exposure has been found, but the child’s close contacts are being given oseltamivir (Tamiflu) as a precaution. Cambodia reported 19 human cases of H5N1 in 2025, eight of which were fatal. Most recent cases in Cambodia have involved a reassortant (2.3.2.1e) between an older H5N1 clade that has circulated in Cambodia since 2014 and the newer clade 2.3.4.4b virus that is circulating globally. Utah reports H5N1 in poultry, cattle In US news, the US Department of Agriculture's Animal and Plant Health Inspection Service (APHIS) reported for the second week in a row an egg farm H5N1 outbreak in Cache County, Utah, affecting 275,100 birds. In the past 30 days, 1.49 million birds have been hit with H5N1 in the United States. APHIS also noted several new detections of avian flu in cattle in Utah and Idaho, with each state reporting four confirmed cases this week. Twenty-six cattle in the past 30 days have been confirmed to be infected with H5N1, raising the year’s total to 81 cases. Moms who receive COVID, flu vaccines during pregnancy more likely to vaccinate their babies, study says News brief July 16, 2026 Meghan Holohan Topics Childhood Vaccines COVID-19 Influenza Vaccines Adult (non-flu) Vaccines Pressmaster / iStock Infants born to mothers vaccinated before, during, or soon after pregnancy for COVID-19 or flu were more likely to receive vaccines than those born to unvaccinated mothers, suggests a study published in Pediatrics. “The results show that maternal vaccination is strongly associated with infant vaccination in the first year of life, especially when vaccination occurred during pregnancy or shortly after delivery,” wrote the study’s authors, from Kaiser Permanente Northern California. “Improving maternal vaccine coverage during those times may improve child vaccine coverage, ensuring continuous protection in early life.” Implications for policymaking, intervention development The researchers looked at 82,533 mother-infant pairs in a retrospective cohort study of babies born from August 18, 2021, to March 31, 2024, to see how mothers’ vaccination status affected their babies’ likelihood of receiving flu or COVID-19 vaccines. The mothers of 32,370 babies (39% of the total) received a COVID-19 vaccine during pregnancy, and 43,806 babies (53%) were born to mothers who received a flu shot while pregnant. Improving maternal vaccine coverage during those times may improve child vaccine coverage, ensuring continuous protection in early life. If moms received a COVID-19 vaccine during pregnancy, their babies were 9.65 times more likely to also receive the COVID-19 vaccine. Moms who received a flu shot had babies who were 3.67 times more likely to also receive one. Babies had a 66.2% probability of getting a flu shot and a 23.4% chance of getting a COVID vaccine before they turned 1 year. “Our findings also suggest that some women may not be comfortable being vaccinated during pregnancy but are willing to vaccinate their infants; differences in attitudes toward vaccinating themselves vs their children have been observed in qualitative studies of parents, although not specifically around pregnancy,” the authors wrote. “Future studies examining the interplay of these factors will be an important part of making policy and developing interventions.” Most measles patients who sought care at Prisma Health in South Carolina were kids News brief July 16, 2026 Sarah Boden Topics Measles Most of the measles patients Prisma Health Greenville Memorial Hospital cared for during South Carolina's recent outbreak were kids aged 10 years or younger. NIH According to a letter from the health system's physicians, published yesterday in the New England Journal of Medicine, Prisma treated 81 people for measles from October 2025 to April 2026. A total of 997 cases were reported to the South Carolina Department of Public Health during the outbreak. The cases were concentrated in the Upstate region, particularly Spartanburg County, where measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccination coverage was 88.9%. The letter notes that at least 73% of those who sought care at Prisma were unvaccinated. Statewide, 93% of cases were among unvaccinated South Carolinians. The most common symptoms observed by Prisma clinicians were fever, cough or other respiratory symptoms, and the characteristic rash of flat red spots. Koplik spots, which are tiny, bluish-white clustered lesions on the inside of the mouth, were reported in a quarter of patients. Severe outcomes, lasting complications None of the 81 patients seen at Prisma Health died. Ten children and three adults were hospitalized, primarily for dehydration, respiratory problems, or pneumonia. Most hospitalized patients were sent home within 72 hours, though the report notes that two had prolonged hospitalizations due to measles encephalitis, a life-threatening inflammation of the brain. One of the two was discharged to an inpatient rehabilitation facility and "as of the time of this report is receiving ongoing outpatient therapy." The other patient recovered but continues to have headaches and a vocal tic. Within a month after being diagnosed with measles, 24 of the patients had concurrent or subsequent infections. The most common infections were pneumonia, a type of ear infection called otitis media, and sinusitis, an inflammation of the sinus tissue.
