B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

当禽流感袭击英国时,成千上万的海鸟死亡。澳大利亚的野生羊群很快也会这样做——《卫报》

When bird flu hit the UK, suffering seabirds died in their thousands. Australia’s wild flocks will soon do the same - The Guardian
新闻简报根据建模和联邦风险评估,澳大利亚面临着 野生鸟类 和 高致病性禽流感 A(H5N1) 谱系 2.3.4.4b 海洋哺乳动物即将大规模灭绝。首次确认的澳大利亚本土鸟类大规模 死亡率 事件发生在南澳大利亚的石灰岩海岸,受影响的是更大的凤头燕鸥。专家警告说,这种病毒可能会在一年内在整个欧洲大陆传播,这与 2022 年以来欧洲所遭受的破坏如出一辙,当时数以万计的海鸟在单个栖息地死亡。近 400 种澳大利亚鸟类和哺乳动物物种被判定为高风险,其中包括标志性鳍足类动物,如濒临灭绝的澳大利亚海狮和澳大利亚毛皮海豹,其风险被归类为极高。虽然证据中没有报告人类病例,但研究人员警告说,非洲大陆独特的鸟类动物群面临着不确定的结果,随着病毒现在在各大洲传播,引发了人们对物种丧失和全球灭绝风险升高的担忧。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布于 2026-08-08;事件时间表跨越 2021 年至 2026 年,当前风险预测将在专家声明的一年内进行。
地点与对象
澳大利亚(全大陆)、南澳大利亚石灰岩海岸(第一次大规模 死亡率 事件)、英国诺福克(历史参考)、亚南极繁殖地(引入来源)。受影响的种群包括野生海鸟(大凤头燕鸥、三明治燕鸥、塘鹅、鲣鸟、鹤、蛇鲈、鸭子、鹅、天鹅、海鸥、燕鸥、斑鹬、鹈鹕、乌鸦、渡鸦、猎鹰、红隼)、鳍足类(海豹和海狮)以及澳大利亚独特的鸟类。
事件
已报告的大规模 死亡率 事件和预计由于 高致病性禽流感 A(H5N1) 谱系 2.3.4.4b 造成的澳大利亚 死亡率 和海洋哺乳动物的大规模死亡,已确认澳大利亚首次发生本地鸟类大规模 死亡率 事件,联邦政府风险分析表明存在高风险物种。该报告综合了专家建模和官方评估。
规模、影响与风险
自 2022 年以来,欧洲单个殖民地就有数万只海鸟死亡;自 2021 年以来,全球有数百万鸟类和哺乳动物被杀害;超过 400 种鸟类和多种哺乳动物受到感染;澳大利亚联邦分析确定近 400 种鸟类和哺乳动物物种处于高风险或更严重的风险;鳍足类物种(新西兰海狗、澳大利亚海狗、澳大利亚海狮)风险极高;预计一年内将在整个非洲大陆传播;全球蔓延加剧了物种丧失和全球范围内灭绝的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
澳大利亚两个月前在迁徙海鸟中检测到 H5N1;联邦政府公布了对潜在威胁的分析;基于全局暴发到WOAH的专家建模;研究确定了高风险鸟类家族;对澳大利亚独特物种的确切影响仍然存在不确定性;证据中没有澳大利亚报告的人类病例;通过 监测 和风险建模隐含的预防措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

When bird flu hit the UK, suffering seabirds died in their thousands. Australia’s wild flocks will soon do the same - The Guardian

News Brief
Australia faces imminent large-scale die-offs of wild birds and marine mammals from highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) lineage 2.3.4.4b, according to modelling and federal risk assessments. The first confirmed mass mortality event in native Australian birds occurred on South Australia’s Limestone Coast, with greater crested terns affected. Experts warn the virus will likely spread continent-wide within a year, mirroring devastation seen in Europe since 2022 where tens of thousands of seabirds perished in single colonies. Nearly 400 Australian bird and mammal species are judged at high risk, including iconic pinnipeds such as the endangered Australian sea lion and Australian fur seal, for which the risk is classified as very high. While no human cases are reported in the evidence, researchers caution that the continent’s unique bird fauna face uncertain outcomes, raising concerns about species loss and elevated global extinction risks as the virus now circulates on every continent.
Original source text (English)

One expert expects the disease will spread across the continent within a year. Photograph: DEA/P. JACCOD/De Agostini/Getty Images When bird flu hit the UK, suffering seabirds died in their thousands. Australia’s wild flocks will soon do the same Australia’s bird population will experience ‘large mortalities’ and risk species loss, modelling based on global outbreaks shows Sign up for climate and environment editor Adam Morton’s free Clear Air newsletter here Sandwich terns are a graceful seabird known for their committed, forceful and spectacular plunging dives into the ocean to catch fish. Thousands of the species started to fall sick across Europe in 2022 with tens of thousands dying as a lineage of the H5N1 bird flu – 2.3.4.4b – spread through the continent’s wild flocks. The effects of the virus on the brain cause the terns to flounder on the ground, craning and straining their necks in a suffering behaviour known as star-gazing. It is difficult to watch. “I remember a colony at Scolt Head Island in Norfolk,” says Prof James Pearce-Higgins, the director of science at the British Trust for Ornithology. “You had adult terns trying to mate with dead terns and chicks begging for food from their dying parents. View image in fullscreen The sandwich tern (pictured) is a close relative of the greater crested tern, which is common in Australia. Photograph: Natural England/Allan Drewitt/PA “It was a real sense of a colony collapse as you watched this transfer of the virus between birds.” Sandwich terns are close relatives of greater crested terns, which are seen all around Australia and were the first native bird to suffer a mass mortality event from the disease in Australia on South Australia’s Limestone Coast. Since the virus re-emerged in Europe in 2021 it has swept over every other continent, killing millions of birds and mammals, and infecting more than 400 bird species as well as mammals – in particular, seals and sea lions. The virus was finally detected in Australia two months ago in migratory seabirds that likely brought the disease from breeding grounds in the subantarctic. Sign up to get climate and environment editor Adam Morton’s Clear Air column as a free newsletter This week, the federal government revealed analysis of the potential threat to Australia’s wildlife which showed almost 400 species of birds and mammals were at “high risk” or worse if they caught the disease. One expert who worked on the analysis, Prof John Woinarski of Charles Darwin University, tells the Guardian he expects the disease will have spread across the continent within a year. The last continent: how deadly bird flu travelled the world before landing on a remote Australian beach Read more Pearce-Higgins says: “I would expect Australia is going to experience large mortalities, like those we have been through, and it’s going to put some species at risk.” Almost half of Australia’s bird species are unique to the continent, meaning that a lot of uncertainty comes with predicting what H5N1 could do to the animal population. It also means the risk of the virus causing planet-wide extinctions is higher now that every continent is affected. Death toll will be ‘confronting’ “We’ve been looking overseas since 2021 and seeing the advance of this virus,” says Dr Sara Ryding, an evolutionary ecologist at Deakin University. “It has been horrible overseas – tens of thousands of birds dying in single colonies. “The concern was, we knew it was going to arrive eventually – how could it not? But we didn’t know what it would look like when it got here.” View image in fullscreen Spending time around bodies of water, where the virus can live for months, can make animals susceptible to bird flu. Photograph: Danielle Connor/Alamy Ryding has led research to project the level of risk from H5N1 for all Australia’s bird families by developing a model based on official reports of the disease from around the globe to the World Organisation for Animal Health. The research suggested the most at-risk families of birds were gannets and boobies (Sulidae), followed in descending order of risk by cranes (Gruidae), darters (Anhingidae), ducks, geese and swans (Anatidae), and gulls, terns and noddies (Laridae). But a host of other bird families – including pelicans, crows, ravens, falcons and kestrels – were all predicted to be susceptible. While many species of those birds are not considered threatened, the sight of large numbers of dead and sick birds, particularly larger species like swans and pelicans, “is going to be confronting,” Ryding says. “I don’t want to cause panic, but we have seen the devastation it has caused overseas and there’s no reason to think it will be any less devastating here,” she says. “I think it will be really bad.” Studies have identified several behaviours that can make animals susceptible to catching the disease, such as scavenging dead and dying animals for food (as do gulls and Tasmanian devils), spending time in dense colonies (like seals and waterbirds) and spending time around bodies of water where the virus can live for weeks or months, or where other infected animals might also be around. ‘Devastating’ for seals and sea lions The virus has been particularly damaging for seals and sea lions. Tens of thousands of seals and sea lions have died in outbreaks in Peru, Chile, Brazil, Uruguay and Argentina and, most recently, on Australia’s subantarctic territory of Heard Island and McDonald Islands. The virus can cause debilitating conditions for the seals and sea lions – known collectively as pinnipeds – with severe neurological and respiratory symptoms. View image in fullscreen Australian fur seals near Maria Island, Tasmania. Because they live in dense groups, seals are particularly susceptible to bird flu. Photograph: Robert Wyatt/Alamy Australia has three resident pinniped species – the New Zealand fur seal, the Australian fur seal and the already endangered Australian sea lion – and government analysis has suggested the risk for all three from the virus is “very high”, meaning “very severe reduction in population size or viability is predicted” if the virus reaches them. Dr Saia Bartes is an Argentinian marine biologist who has just completed her PhD on Australian fur seals at Deakin University and has helped study declines in South American sea lions after the virus struck there. View image in fullscreen A young Australian fur seal near Montague Island, NSW. Seal numbers are still recovering from commercial hunting. Photograph: By Wildestanimal/Getty Images “It was devastating for them. In north Patagonia, we lost a third of the sea lions,” she says. Because pinnipeds often share space with infected birds, and tend to be in tightly packed groups, Bartes says they are particularly susceptible. Pinniped numbers are still recovering from commercial hunting, she says, and “they could start declining again really quickly, which is happening in South America” if the virus found them. Back in the UK, Pearce-Higgins says there is some hope. Their experience of collaboration with conservation groups, volunteers and local authorities through weekly and later monthly meetings had been critical in the response. And there were some signs the impacts of the virus may be slowing. “The hope is that because a lot of these birds are long-lived, the population could be more resilient. But we still don’t really know the probabilities of future outbreaks,” he says. “It might look really bad for a few years, but the hope is that [Australia] can focus on the conservation of what is left.” Explore more on these topics Bird flu Birds Wildlife Endangered species features Share Reuse this content Most viewed Massachusetts teen accused of killing mother and brother used ChatGPT US conducted ‘mass spying campaign’ against leftwing groups and anti-ICE protesters, records reveal Nigel Farage set for decisive but arguably empty victory in Clacton Donald Trump empowers US private companies to conduct cyber-attacks ‘Show the necessary respect’: German athlete Kolbe slams racist abuse of teammates More on this story More on this story Native birds in captivity to be vaccinated against H5 bird flu as Australia prepares for more mass mortalities Jean hoped bird flu wouldn’t come to Australia. Time
Published 2026-08-08; event timeline spans 2021–2026 with current risk projection within one year from expert statements.
Location and population
Australia (continent-wide), South Australia’s Limestone Coast (first mass mortality event), Norfolk UK (historical reference), subantarctic breeding grounds (source of introduction). Affected populations include wild seabirds (greater crested terns, sandwich terns, gannets, boobies, cranes, darters, ducks, geese, swans, gulls, terns, noddies, pelicans, crows, ravens, falcons, kestrels), pinnipeds (seals and sea lions), and unique Australian bird species.
Event
Reported mass mortality events and projected large-scale die-offs of wild birds and marine mammals in Australia due to highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) lineage 2.3.4.4b, with confirmed first native bird mass mortality event in Australia and federal government risk analysis indicating high-risk species. The report synthesizes expert modelling and official assessments.
Scale, impact and risk
Tens of thousands of seabirds died in single European colonies since 2022; millions of birds and mammals killed globally since 2021; over 400 bird species and multiple mammal species infected; Australian federal analysis identifies almost 400 bird and mammal species at high risk or worse; pinniped species (New Zealand fur seal, Australian fur seal, Australian sea lion) at very high risk; projected continent-wide spread within one year; risk of species loss and planet-wide extinctions heightened by global spread.
Response, status and uncertainty
Australia detected H5N1 two months prior in migratory seabirds; federal government revealed analysis of potential threat; expert modelling based on global outbreaks to WOAH; research identifies high-risk bird families; uncertainty remains about exact impact on unique Australian species; no reported human cases in Australia in the evidence; prevention measures implied through surveillance and risk modelling.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

H5N1 引发澳大利亚流行病 - 雅虎

H5N1 sparks a very Australian epidemic - Yahoo
新闻简报Australia is experiencing its first H5N1 highly pathogenic avian influenza outbreak, confirmed in wild seabirds since June 2026. The virus now threatens iconic and endangered native wildlife, including Tasmanian devils, dingoes, and the platypus, with a government assessment warning nearly one in five of more than 2,000 native species faces high risk. To mitigate impacts, authorities will begin vaccinating captive endangered species such as the orange-bellied parrot and regent honeyeater. While the human health risk is currently assessed as low, the virus has caused over 1,000 human cases globally since the late 1990s, with nearly 50% mortality. The poultry industry, valued at $4.1 billion annually, remains under threat, prompting calls for stringent biosecurity measures to prevent further spread.
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 8 月 13 日发布;事件于 2026 年 6 月开始,风险持续存在。
地点与对象
澳大利亚(全国),重点关注独特的野生动物,包括塔斯马尼亚袋獾、袋鼬、野狗、鸭嘴兽、橙腹鹦鹉、摄政吸蜜鸟和黑凤头鹦鹉。
事件
H5N1 高致病性禽流感 已进入澳大利亚,自 2026 年 6 月起在野生海鸟中得到证实,有记录表明对标志性和濒危本土动物存在风险,并且未来可能传播到家禽;澳大利亚政府正在启动针对圈养濒危物种的 疫苗接种 行动。
规模、影响与风险
Over 200 wild seabirds infected; government assessment warns nearly one in five of more than 2,000 native species faces high risk; iconic species at risk of severe population reduction or extinction; poultry industry valued at $4.1 billion annually is under threat; human health risk currently assessed as low, but pandemic potential remains if human-to-human transmission emerges; historically, over 1,000 human cases with nearly 50% mortality since late 1990s.
应对、状态与不确定性
Australian government plans vaccination campaign for endangered species in captivity starting with rare birds; authorities acknowledge inability to stop spread among wild birds; $113 million AUD invested in preparedness including pandemic vaccine stockpiles; biosecurity emphasized to protect poultry; ongoing monitoring and risk assessment for spillover to domestic flocks and potential adaptation to humans.
B routine priorityPublic-health news

H5N1 sparks a very Australian epidemic - Yahoo

News Brief
Australia is experiencing its first H5N1 highly pathogenic avian influenza outbreak, confirmed in wild seabirds since June 2026. The virus now threatens iconic and endangered native wildlife, including Tasmanian devils, dingoes, and the platypus, with a government assessment warning nearly one in five of more than 2,000 native species faces high risk. To mitigate impacts, authorities will begin vaccinating captive endangered species such as the orange-bellied parrot and regent honeyeater. While the human health risk is currently assessed as low, the virus has caused over 1,000 human cases globally since the late 1990s, with nearly 50% mortality. The poultry industry, valued at $4.1 billion annually, remains under threat, prompting calls for stringent biosecurity measures to prevent further spread.
Original source text (English)
H5N1 bird flu has reached Australia's shores and is threatening to infect some of the country's most unique wildlife. Australasia is the last continent to be hit by the highly infectious avian influenza H5N1 , a new strain of which has been circulating worldwide since 2020. Since recording its first case in June, more than 200 wild seabirds in Australia have been infected, with fears that the highly infectious flu could quickly 'jump' to other species. While H5N1 has infected animals from foxes to sea lions in other parts of the world in recent years, the epidemic now threatens some of Australia's most iconic wildlife – many of which are found nowhere else on earth. An Australian government assessment of more than 2,000 native species warned that nearly one in five faces a high risk from the deadly H5N1 bird flu, with hundreds of unique birds and mammals vulnerable to severe population reductions or even extinction. Those animals include iconic species like the Tasmanian devils, quolls, and dingoes. Other aquatic mammals, including the platypus, which is only found in Australia, are at risk of infection through contact with bird droppings in shared wetlands and coastal environments. Tasmanian devils are an endangered species - HANDOUT/Aussie Ark/AFP via Getty Images Dingoes are among the mammals that are unique to Australia - iStockphoto "This could be quite devastating to what is a unique ecosystem with endangered populations," Professor Ian Brown, Head of Avian Virology at The Pirbright Institute, told The Telegraph. "It's been a waiting game [...] I don't think that any are that surprised that the virus has got to Australia now," Prof Ashley C. Banyard, an avian virology expert at the government's Animal and Plant Health Agency, said. "I think people have been expecting it [the virus] to reach Australia as several bird species that travel to that region have long-distance migratory pathways from areas where the virus has been detected previously, and we continue to detect positives on British Overseas Territories in the sub-Antarctic region where these species migrate from", said Prof Banyard. The government is set to begin a vaccination campaign in the coming weeks to protect some of these endangered species that are conserved in captivity – beginning with rare birds like the orange-bellied parrot, the regent honeyeater, and Western Australia's black cockatoos. "We've been very clear that we cannot stop the H5 bird flu from spreading amongst wild birds, but we can take action to protect the species most at risk, and the vaccination is now part of that response," Australia's Agriculture Minister Julie Collins said in a statement on Tuesday. "Obviously, Australia wants to protect these species; they're of high conservation concern, and they want to avoid this virus ripping through them," said Prof Banyard. "But I think the bigger picture is how do you deal with the introductions from wild birds, which we've all suffered with for many years across Europe and elsewhere. "Ultimately biosecurity is essential to prevention in poultry species and with wild bird cases increasing in the region, stringent biosecurity will be critical in preventing this becoming an even bigger problem," he added. Since 2022, H5N1 has spread at a rapid pace across the world, decimating millions of domestic and wild bird populations as well as infecting a wide range of animals, from otters to seals to lions, tigers, and bears. It has had a huge impact on the farming industry and sent the price of eggs and poultry meat soaring as flocks are culled to try to contain the spread of the virus. In Britain and the EU, almost 48 million birds were culled between 2021 and 2022 alone at the height of the bird-flu season. This risk now threatens the Australian poultry industry, which contributes roughly $4.1 billion to the regional agricultural industry every year. "It hasn't hit poultry [in Australia] yet. If it does get into flocks, it's always going to be problematic. But I would imagine flocks in Australia and New Zealand are quite spread apart, and so the risk of lateral spread is probably lower [than in the UK]," said Prof Banyard. The virus also poses a threat to human health, and it has long been feared that H5N1 could spark the next pandemic, should it develop the ability to spread between people. More than 1,000 people have contracted the virus since it first emerged in the late 1990s, mostly through close contact with sickened animals, almost half of whom have died. Australian authorities currently deem the risk to human health as "low", and have invested $113 million Australian dollars (£59 million) into preparedness activities, including boosting national stockpiles of pandemic flu vaccines, should the virus gain the ability to spread person-to-person. Response, status and uncertainty

吉恩希望禽流感不会来到澳大利亚。然后它来了——她看到一只燕鸥“颤抖着摇摆”——《卫报》

Jean hoped bird flu wouldn’t come to Australia. Then it arrived – and she saw a tern ‘trembling and swaying’ - The Guardian
新闻简报南澳大利亚州已确认野生海鸟中首次出现 暴发 H5 禽流感疫情,罗布附近的 Baudin Rocks 报告有超过 80 只死亡和患病的大凤头燕鸥。该州政府与保护组织一起动员了一个观鸟志愿者网络来监测和报告患病鸟类,袋鼠岛上的多只凤头燕鸥的病毒检测呈阳性。当局敦促公众不要处理生病或死亡的野生动物,并拨打专门的热线报告目击事件。暴发 威胁着包括企鹅和海豚在内的近 400 种澳大利亚物种,引发了人们对生态破坏的担忧。虽然 监测 处于活动状态,但 暴发 的全部范围仍在调查中。
查看新闻五要素
时间
Published on 2026-08-13; event occurred in early August 2026 following South Australia's confirmation of H5 bird flu on 2026-08-07.
地点与对象
澳大利亚南澳大利亚;特别是石灰岩海岸和袋鼠岛的罗布。受影响的种群包括野生海鸟(大凤头燕鸥)、南澳大利亚鸟类观察网络的志愿者和当地观鸟社区。
事件
南澳大利亚确认首例野生海鸟感染 H5 禽流感病例,引发社区主导的 监测 应对措施。志愿者接受了识别和报告病禽的培训,并在多只凤头燕鸥中确认了 H5 阳性结果。该活动得到官方监测和志愿者报告的证实。
规模、影响与风险
博丹岩 (Baudin Rocks) 首次在大凤头燕鸥中发现大规模 死亡率 事件,据报道有 80 多只鸟类死亡和患病。袋鼠岛多只凤头燕鸥检测出H5禽流感呈阳性。暴发 威胁着近 400 种澳大利亚物种,包括企鹅和海豚。由于对 野生鸟类 种群和更广泛的生态系统的生态影响,风险被描述为潜在严重。
应对、状态与不确定性
南澳大利亚州政府与保护组织合作,启动了志愿者 监测 网络来报告患病鸟类。敦促公众不要触摸生病或死亡的动物,并通过紧急动物疾病热线报告。监测 正在进行中,其他州正在考虑类似的模型。传播的整体范围和长期生态影响仍然存在不确定性。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Jean hoped bird flu wouldn’t come to Australia. Then it arrived – and she saw a tern ‘trembling and swaying’ - The Guardian

News Brief
South Australia has confirmed its first outbreak of H5 bird flu in wild seabirds, with over 80 dead and sick greater crested terns reported at Baudin Rocks near Robe. The state’s government, alongside conservation groups, has mobilized a volunteer network of birders to monitor and report sick birds, with multiple crested terns on Kangaroo Island testing positive for the virus. Authorities urge the public not to handle sick or dead wildlife and to report sightings to a dedicated hotline. The outbreak threatens nearly 400 Australian species, including penguins and dolphins, raising concerns about ecological damage. While surveillance is active, the full scope of the outbreak remains under investigation.
Original source text (English)

Photograph: Jean Turner Jean Turner and David Potter, members of the South Australia Bird Observers Network. Photograph: Jean Turner Jean hoped bird flu wouldn’t come to Australia. Then it arrived – and she saw a tern ‘trembling and swaying’ Volunteers are on the lookout for sick birds as outbreak shatters ‘fairytale belief’ that disease would not reach Australia Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast Bird enthusiast Jean Turner hoped avian flu would never arrive at her doorstep. At her home on Kangaroo Island in South Australia, bossy fairy wrens chirp at her from the deck, grey currawongs clumsily fall into her bird bath, and fluffy, hooded plover chicks emerge from speckled eggs in front of her binoculars. But online, images of Argentinian coastlines strewn with dead seal pups and researchers lining up rows of dead seabirds on Antarctic islands offered a warning of what could come. ‘We expected it, but now it’s here’: on the frontline of Australia’s bird flu outbreak, people are distressed Read more So when SA confirmed its first case of the deadly H5 bird flu strain, just over 200km away at Robe on the Limestone Coast, Turner was prepared to become a different kind of birder. “We had this fairytale belief that maybe we wouldn’t get it here,” she says. “But it’s here and now things are going to get harder and more confronting.” She is one of more than 120 volunteers who have signed up for the Bird Observers Network, which allows birders to contribute to the state’s bird flu response. The partnership between the SA government, Conservation Council SA, Birds SA and Birdlife Australia trains volunteers to identify and report sick birds that may be showing signs associated with H5 bird flu. Since the network’s first session, Turner has already reported a sick greater crested tern, which was resting at the edge of a flock. While the other birds shuffled or flew around as she tried to get a better look, the sick animal did not respond. “It was standing on the jetty, trembling and swaying a bit,” Turner says. “It didn’t look well, it just looked really miserable.” Compared with the non-bird-obsessed general public, birders can offer greater detail when reporting potentially infected birds, says Steven Langley, the president of Birds SA. Sign up for the Breaking News Australia email They can often recognise species more accurately, use their binoculars to observe possible abnormalities within a flock, and notice when birds they might see on their routine walks go missing. “We all just want to be able to do whatever we can to make a difference,” Langley says. “When you see these distressing images from overseas, you’re implanting them into your mind and on to the places and the birds that you have got a great deal of affection for.” skip past newsletter promotion after newsletter promotion Volunteers generally don’t receive further correspondence after they have made a submission but multiple crested terns found on Kangaroo Island have been tested and returned confirmed positive results for H5 bird flu since Turner’s report. The grim task of reporting dead wildlife is a familiar one to many SA birders after the toxic algal bloom which engulfed 20,000 sq km of coast, killing millions of sea creatures and becoming one of the worst marine mass mortalities in the world. Though Turner says the scenes from the bloom were confronting, she worries bird flu has the potential to be even worse. The first mass mortality event of the outbreak was detected last week among greater created terns at Baudin Rocks, just across the beach from Robe, where more than 80 dead and sick birds were spotted. Penguins and dolphins among nearly 400 Australian species under threat from bird flu as ‘true extent of disaster’ revealed Read more Langley says the impending disaster makes it even more important to take other actions to protect birds, including mitigating predator risks, reducing disturbance and providing and protecting more habitat. “When you lose a huge proportion of your breeding birds to bird flu, every single bird that survives is really, really valuable,” he says. “We need to be reducing disturbance to these birds.” For now, the SA government says it is continuing engagement with the community, the agricultural industry and the wildlife sector to bolster surveillance. The environment minister, Emily Bourke, thanked the birding community for helping with surveillance and says other states and territories were interested in setting up similar models. Birders and the general public who come across dead or sick animals have been urged not to touch them but instead to report them to the Emergency Animal Disease Hotline on on 1800 675 888, and by taking a photo and noting the location. Published on 2026-08-13; event occurred in early August 2026 following South Australia's confirmation of H5 bird flu on 2026-08-07.
Location and population
South Australia, Australia; specifically Robe on the Limestone Coast and Kangaroo Island. Affected population includes wild seabirds (greater crested terns), volunteers from the South Australia Bird Observers Network, and local birding communities.
Event
South Australia confirmed its first case of H5 bird flu in wild seabirds, prompting a community-led surveillance response. Volunteers are trained to identify and report sick birds, with confirmed positive results for H5 in multiple crested terns. The event is confirmed by official surveillance and volunteer reports.
Scale, impact and risk
First mass mortality event detected among greater crested terns at Baudin Rocks, with over 80 dead and sick birds reported. Multiple crested terns on Kangaroo Island tested positive for H5 bird flu. Risk is described as potentially severe due to the ecological impact on wild bird populations and the broader ecosystem.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

印第安纳州家禽宣布不含 禽流感 - WATTPoultry.com

Indiana poultry declared free of avian influenza - WATTPoultry.com
新闻简报自 2026 年 5 月以来,印第安纳州的家禽业在商业鸡群中没有发现新的 高致病性禽流感 (HPAI) 病例,因此宣布该州的家禽业不受 禽流感 影响。此前,该州在 2026 年记录的与 HPAI 相关的鸡群损失比美国任何其他州都多,突显了这一里程碑的重要性。WATTPoultry.com 于 2026 年 8 月 13 日报道的这一声明反映了一段持续没有新感染病例的时期,尽管证据没有具体说明病例数、暴露数量或除了没有新病例之外的声明依据。这一发展遵循了 2026 年中期美国商业家禽中高致病性禽流感活动有限的更广泛趋势。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表于2026-08-13;截至 2026 年 5 月的事件状态,此后没有新病例 (N2)。
地点与对象
美国印第安纳州 (N2)。
事件
印第安纳州家禽业自 2026 年 5 月 (N2) 以来没有出现新的 高致病性禽流感 (HPAI) 鸡群感染,因此宣布摆脱 禽流感。
规模、影响与风险
2026 年,印第安纳州的 HPAI 鸡群损失比美国任何其他州都多,但提供的证据中没有报告病例数、死亡或暴露情况 (N2)。
应对、状态与不确定性
宣布无高致病性禽流感的依据是自 2026 年 5 月以来没有出现新的感染;证据中没有详细说明进一步的调查、测试或应对措施 (N2)。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Indiana poultry declared free of avian influenza - WATTPoultry.com

News Brief
Indiana’s poultry industry has been declared free of avian influenza after no new highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI) cases were detected in commercial flocks since May 2026. The state had previously recorded more HPAI-related flock losses than any other U.S. state in 2026, underscoring the significance of the milestone. The declaration, reported by WATTPoultry.com on August 13, 2026, reflects a sustained period without new infections, though the evidence does not specify case counts, exposure numbers, or the basis for the declaration beyond the absence of new cases. The development follows a broader U.S. trend of limited HPAI activity in commercial poultry during mid-2026.

‘Avian influenza, goat pox vaccines to be produced commercially’

News Brief
Latest Bangladesh Business World Sports Opinion Politics Diplomacy More E-Paper ☀️ 🌐 English বাংলা Russian Hindi Chinese Trending Presidential Election Gold Price Tarique Rahman US-Iran Talks Saudi-Houthi Conflict × Today's Newspaper Latest World Cup Magazine 2026 Election 2026 Follow Us Print: 14 Aug 2026 Health ‘Avian influenza, goat pox vaccines to be produced commercially’ Daily Sun Report, Dhaka Published: 12 Aug 2026, 12:00 AM Updated: 12 Aug 2026, 03:30 AM Share Share options Print Copy link Facebook X WhatsApp Linkedin A A Fisheries and Livestock and Agriculture Minister Mohammad Amin Ur Rashid has said vaccines for avian influenza and goat pox developed by the Bangladesh Livestock Research Institute (BLRI) will be produced commercially and supplied to farmers. He made the remarks as the chief guest at a ceremony at the ministry’s conference room on Tuesday, where BLRI formally handed over the vaccines to the Department of Livestock Services (DLS) for commercial production and wider distribution among farmers. Referring to the livestock sector as an important component of the country’s agricultural economy, the minister said farmers could suffer significant financial losse.
Original source text (English)
Latest Bangladesh Business World Sports Opinion Politics Diplomacy More E-Paper ☀️ 🌐 English বাংলা Russian Hindi Chinese Trending Presidential Election Gold Price Tarique Rahman US-Iran Talks Saudi-Houthi Conflict × Today's Newspaper Latest World Cup Magazine 2026 Election 2026 Follow Us Print: 14 Aug 2026 Health ‘Avian influenza, goat pox vaccines to be produced commercially’ Daily Sun Report, Dhaka Published: 12 Aug 2026, 12:00 AM Updated: 12 Aug 2026, 03:30 AM Share Share options Print Copy link Facebook X WhatsApp Linkedin A A Fisheries and Livestock and Agriculture Minister Mohammad Amin Ur Rashid has said vaccines for avian influenza and goat pox developed by the Bangladesh Livestock Research Institute (BLRI) will be produced commercially and supplied to farmers. He made the remarks as the chief guest at a ceremony at the ministry’s conference room on Tuesday, where BLRI formally handed over the vaccines to the Department of Livestock Services (DLS) for commercial production and wider distribution among farmers. Referring to the livestock sector as an important component of the country’s agricultural economy, the minister said farmers could suffer significant financial losses if diseases affecting poultry and livestock were not controlled. Diseases such as avian influenza, in particular, can have a major impact on poultry production and farm management, he said, stressing the need to strengthen the country’s capacity to develop and produce effective vaccines domestically. The vaccines developed through domestic technology and research would help control diseases in the livestock and poultry sectors, reduce production costs and lessen dependence on imported vaccines, he said. The minister said BLRI scientists and researchers has developed the vaccines using domestic technology and materials after years of research, testing and overcoming various challenges. “If an innovation developed in a laboratory does not benefit ordinary people, the full benefits of that research cannot be realised,” he said, adding that the priority now was to take research-based innovations to commercial production and ensure their use at the farmer level. Highlighting various government initiatives to support agriculture and farmers, the minister said a large number of people in the country were directly or indirectly involved in agriculture. “If agriculture develops, the country’s economy will also become stronger,” he said, adding that the government was working to strengthen farmers and the agricultural sector to advance the overall economy. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

澳大利亚开始为本土鸟类接种 H5N1 疫苗 - The Poultry Site

Australia to begin vaccinating native birds against H5N1 - The Poultry Site
新闻简报澳大利亚启动了一项针对本土鸟类 H5N1 的 疫苗接种 计划,重点关注濒临灭绝的圈养物种。此举旨在防止可能威胁生物多样性的禽流感 暴发。有关 疫苗 配方、优先考虑的特定鸟类物种以及实施时间表的详细信息仍不清楚。监测 该病毒在野生动物中传播的差距和不确定性凸显了进一步研究的必要性。这一积极措施凸显了人们对生态系统中人畜共患流感风险日益增长的担忧。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发布时间:2026-08-11。事件:正在进行的针对本地鸟类对抗 H5N1 的 疫苗接种 计划。没有提供具体的时间范围。
地点与对象
以本土鸟类为目标,特别是濒临灭绝的圈养物种。没有报告人口数据。
事件
澳大利亚开始为本土鸟类接种 H5N1 疫苗。家禽网站报道。状态:已确认计划启动,但具体细节(例如 疫苗 类型、优先物种)没有详细证据。
规模、影响与风险
没有提供病例、死亡或暴露计数。风险:如果 H5N1 病毒传播,脆弱鸟类可能会灭绝。没有说明对卫生系统或运营的影响。
应对、状态与不确定性
计划实施已确认。不确定性:范围(目标物种)、疫苗 功效和 监测 差距。没有提及对鸟类的测试、隔离或接触者追踪。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Australia to begin vaccinating native birds against H5N1 - The Poultry Site

News Brief
Australia has initiated a vaccination program targeting native birds against H5N1, focusing on captive species at risk of extinction. The move aims to prevent avian influenza outbreaks that could threaten biodiversity. Details on the vaccine formulation, specific bird species prioritized, and implementation timelines remain unclear. Surveillance gaps and uncertainties about the virus's spread among wildlife highlight the need for further research. This proactive measure underscores growing concerns about zoonotic influenza risks in ecosystems.
Original source text (English)
